SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

SLEEP INDUCED BY ELECTRICITY.

For some years Professor Ledue, of Nantes, has been experimenting with inter- mittent electricity at low pressure, and ad- ministered at frequent intervals, as a means of inducing sleep in animals. He finds that sleep can be induced by this means, and that during such sleep the arterial pressure in- creases. If the electric pressure is then in- creased the different functions of the body are suspended one by one until death takes place.

OUR TWO ATMOSPHERES.

The lower atmosphere and the upper atmo-. sphere are believed by Professor J. Hann to be two very different gaseous mixtures. Ab the earth's surface tire compcsition is: Nitro- gen, 78.03; oxygen, 2099; argon, 094; car- bonic acid,, 003; hydrogenA 0.01; neon, 00015; helium, 000015; krykton, 0-00010. At a height of 20 kilometres (1.2-43 miles) ho fuads the nitrogen increased to §4.34 per cent., with 15.19 of oxygen. At 100 kilometres the hydrogen seems increased to 99-45 per cent., with 0'453 of helium and only 0-og9, of nitrogen.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MUTTON BIRD.

The mutton bird (puffinus tenuirostris) was so named by the early navigators of Bass Strait on account of the mutton-like flavour of its flesh. Mr. Frank Dullen, in "The Cruise of the Cachalot;" says that when grilled it smells exactly like roast mutton, but that the taste is more like kippered herring. In certain parts of New Zealand, he tells us, it is. the chief flesh food. The mutton bird has maintained its reputation, ancl it is in some danger of extermination.

SOLAR CYCLONES. Under the title ef "Cyclones on the Sun," the Scientific American has given an accouni of some remarkable investigations on sun. spots carried out at the Carnegie Solar Oh servatory, California, by Professor George E. Hale. The result of these is to chew that • the disturbances in the sun which give rise to sun-spots are of the nature of cyclones. A • -whirling inrush of dark and comparatively -cool hydrogen is one of the accompanying phenomena. By means of the spectro-helio graph , invented by Professor Hale, it is pes- sible to examine s2parately the light due to different incandescent elements in the sun. For by means of a spe.ctroscope the light, say from glowing calcium, can be separated from that due to hydrogen. By means of a second slit the light from one of these alone can be admitted for further examination.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
PHOTOGRAPHS OF SUN SPOTS.

A photograph of the Sun in the light of glowing calcium alone could thus be ob- tained. To study a sun-spot completely, how- lever, it was BecesEary to photograph it in hydrogen light alone. And here a difficulty arose, for a large portion of the spectrum of .hydrogen is at the red end of the spectrum, and the ordinary photographic plate is not sensitive to red light. Mr. R. J. Wallace, of Yerkes Observatory, however, found that by bathing c, an ordinary plate in a certain solu- tion it became so. Photographs of sun-spots were, therefore, taken on plates thus treated in the manner indicated above.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
HYDROGEN WHIRLPOOLS,

The photographs chewed that 'sun-spots are ,centres of attraction drawing in the hydrogen 11)f the solar atmosphere in rapid whirling mo- tion. An early ohservation flocculi of dark cool hydrogen. Later theee were seen to be set in lA-hit-ling motion, ,and to be sucked into a sort of maelstrom at the rate of sixty miles a second. The next morning the dark flocculi were seen to be replaced by masses of heated hydrogen. This is the first observm- tion of the kind ever Trade, and throws a nes light on the nature of sun-spots.

GROWING PERIODS OF TREES. The length of the growing period of apple, pear, cherry, and plum trees was studied by an experiment station for two yew's. By careful measurements of selected branches at intervals of two to four days the growth was determined.. After June Ist there was no increase in the growth of the pear, nor ,he sherry after May 27th. With the apple the branch growth ceased after June 4th, and with the plum June 23rd. Examination of several hundred other orchard trees during July and August showed no indications of fur- ther growth. During the second season no growth on any of the trees occurred after July 10th, while the pear tree growth ceasea June 18th, and with the apple and plum June 27th. Other trees in the same orchard continued to grow until October Ist, at which tithe most of the terminal buds examined appeared to be plump and fully formed. This second growth on the other trees in the orchards began about July 15th, and out of 325 trees examined 06 per cent. of the trees on culti- vated soil and 211 per cent. of those in sod made a second growth.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MYSTERY OF COMETS.

There is perhaps no other astronomical phenomenon capable of stirring the minds of the mass of mankind so profoundly as the apparition of a great comet, says the Scien- tific American. The psychologic effects of a total eclipse of the sun have at times been extreme. The same may be said of those wonderful displays of meteors in 1833 and 1866, .when for a time it seemed as if all the stars were plunging, or were just about to plunge, upon the earth. But eclipses and meteoric showers are very transient, and whatever the consternation they inspire it is soon past. A comet, however, may hold a huge and brilliant sword of fire in the sky for days and weeks—appalling the minds of men with the fear of some impending and

===== 0002601_19090102_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
INEVITABLB CATASTROPHE.

It may be said that the progress of science justifies, the laying aside of apprehensions of calamity from. this source: This is in part true. But not altogether. The probability of a collision is quite remote, it is true. Stilt :t is within the range of possibility. It may be said that a collision would work no great effect. One justification for this lies in the fact that even the central portion of some comets appears to be extremely tenuous, per- mitting faint stars to be seen through thou- sands of miles of the cometary material. But this is not known to be a necessary attribute of comets in general. In fact, the mass of no comet has ever been accurately determined. So, for aught we know, there may be comets whose nuclei are not only large but massive. A collision with such a visitor from the abyss of space would be disastrous in the highest degree.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
SEEING THINGS AT NIGHT.

Everyone must at times have asked himself why familiar objects in a dim light tend to assume fantastic and oftentimes alarming ap- pearances. The explanation is to be found in the special conditions of night vision, says the British Medical Journal. The pupils are widely dilated, and, as in the photographic lens with a large diaphragm, the apparatus of accommodation can only focus for one plane. As the faculty of estimating dis- tances is in great measure lost in the ob- scurity, we eannot focus with precision, and a blurred, uncertain outline is thrown upon the retina. Then, too, colours viewed in a fading light lose their distinguishing hue in a fixed sequence until a point is reached at which everything becomes of

ONE UNIFORM GREY TINT. It follows that the images which are trans- mitted to the visual centres are profoundly modified in colour and outline, and as they enter the eye through the widely-dilated pupil ,at an altogether unusual angle the movement of locomotion gives them a peculiar mobility Now, one relies on experience for the in- terpretation of sensorial impressions, and when these present themselves suddenly in an unusual form they create a feeling of insecu- rity which finds expression in mental per.. turbation and more or less violent motor im- pulsion. In fact, the subject finds himself in the position of a horse which sees a rapidly advancing automobile for the first time and does not know what to make of it. Imagina- tion aiding, these blurred, mobile, and uncer- tain images are susceptible of the most phan- tasmaaoric interpretation, and in Persons who are not accustomed to control sensorial im- pressions by the exercise, of the intelligance the impressions are accepted as realities ad acted upon accordingly.

WOMEN'S CHT.

Watch-night Services.—There is no mention in the Prayer Book of the watch-night service, which John Wesley first introduced into the Methodist Church in 1761-62. as a celebration of the passing of the year. , After that the annual viatchesight service became a feature of Methodism; and in later times it has been adopted by other churches. in a few churches the service has been continued in the past year or two, on the ground that it has been made the occasicn for irreverent behaviour, and it has been put forward that the services are sensational. But upon the whole there is reason to believe that a good deal of good is effected on these occasions when there are all the possibilities of the preacher's words having particularly great power. Probably most of us have determined that the New Year shall see some little improvement in our ways, and there is no doubt that the solemnities of the watch-night service help a great many people to resolve upon some higher ideals of conduct. The National Anthem.--The National Anthem in now ! played by military bands in about half the time it has • hitherto taken. The keine air is used in Germany and Denmark, but it is singular that, not only our own, but most of the national hymns of Europe have emanated from obscure poets, and comparatively unknown com- posers. By some authorities our national anthem is attributed to Henry Carey, the poet and musician who was the author of "Sally in our Alley" but there seems little doubt that it was in use before his time•l69o to 1743. Probably not otie person in a thousand could repeat the Anthem, of which one verse, usually suffices, but it is not altogether innocent of meaningless doggerel, and could certainly be revised with advan- tage both in thyme and sentiment. The Pantomirnes.—There is a sameness about Christ- mas pantomimes as there is about Christmas dinners. ; Now and again we have an original title such as the Glasgow pantomime " Diccory, Diccory, Dok " but mostly they ese Cinderellap, Dick Whittingtons.Aladdins, Blue Beards and so on. Taking one year with another, Cinderella is the favourite theme not that the story in the back ground makes much difference, It matters little whether it is Blue Beard or Mother Goose, so long as it gives the excuse for spectacle, and the farrago of topical jokes and nonsense, that are so popular with the public. "Mother Goose "by the way was not a legendary person, but one in real life, a Mrs. Isaac Goose, whose nursery songs which she wrote for. her grandchildren, were first published in 1716. The Game Shooting.—President Roosevelt and his wife, who are going to pay us a visit this year, both know London very well, and in fact they were married there, at St. George's, Hanover Square, in 1886. The President is going on a big-game shooting expedition ' in Central Africa, for which the camp arrangements are being prepared in London' and later on Mrs. Roosevelt ,is to join her husband at Khartoum on his return. He I will not have things all his own way in Central Africa, as Lady Constance Stewart-Richardson will also be leaving in the Spring on a shooting expedition to the • Congo. She will make the first part of the journey Tin Mombassa to Uganda, a route Mr. Winston Churchill graphically describes in his book "My African Journey,' and from thence it becomes a very adven- tuyous expedition involving not only risks and hard- ships, tut also dangers from hostile tribes, once the ,area of British influence is passed. This however is not Lady Constauce's first expedition of this kind. She , is a splendid shot, and has many trophies of her travels, all of which fell to her own gun. She was formerly I Lady Constance Mackenzie and married Sir Edward Stewart-Richardson some five years ago. "Trial marriages."—One does not usually regard , Russia as n free and advanced country, and it is a little • surprising to see a Women's Congresfi there, discussing • and advocating" trial marriages." It is from America where it is almost as easy to get divorced as to get , married, that one would expect such ideas to come, but we have had our own reformers notably Mrs. George ' Meredith and others, who are in favour of a contract , for a certain number of years, to he terminated or ; rerewed like a lease. Then there was the lady who advocated the system of two "tries " at matrimony, allowing either party to break the knot at the end of three years. but affording them no release from a second marriage. They would he allowed one mistake, like the dog which used•to IA given his first bite, but having had the chance to acquire matrimonial wisdom there would be no escape from the second venture- There used to be an old custom in Scotland which per- mitted a bride, if found unsatisfactory, to he returned , within a year, but the suggestion of trial marriages" ' savours too much of harbarism,to be seriously discussed and it would of course work out to fatal disadvantage Ifor women. Farthings and the Sales.—That coin of the realm— the farthing—usually despised and rejected, is an important factor at sale time. It is, of course, tacked on to everything, from a pocket handkerchief to a coat !and skirt, yet one rarely catches sight of the real thing. 'lt becomes indeed a mast covetable coin, as at few , places can one get a farthing in change. Begged courteously to accept a small packet of pins instead, it lis difficult to refuse, and one acquires many prickly packages in the course of a morning's shopping. I Forthcoming Bargains.—The greatest bargain at the alles promises to be the blouse with baggy sleeves. This-is likely to be marked down at a quite ridiculous price, as shopland must get rid of all such stock and promptly. The e.sperienced home worker will be able to tell at a glance what sleeves she can make, or replace with new, and undoebtedly many a subsequent up-to- date blouse will thus be attained at very small cost. Then about remnants. The present idea is, for the blouse to match the skirt in colour, though it should be lighter in tone. If possible the underskirt should be of the same hoe as the blouse. This notion is likely to prevail well into the spring, and should be bourne in mind by the remnant hunter. An Economical Idea.—Not every woman who aspires to dress nicely can afford a set of silk petticoats, but a set of frills might often be possible of attainment. For instance at this season of the year, a well-fitting flannel skirt in stripes of soft grey, could be worn, cut to reach a little below the knees only, and a close row of tiny loops worked all round very evenly a little above the knees. Then several frills could be made, of any desired length, to the top of which a close row of small hooks could be sewn, and in this manlier any frill could be easily and speedily attached to, or , detached from the skirt, the entire cost amounting to little, comparatively. Travelling Coats.—ln the matter of travelling coats, ' there is little new worth noting. Some of the latest specimens it is true are particularly elaborated, and , warranted to perform wonderful feats and quick changes, hut really the quite simple and plain varieties 8713 the best. These, for the most part, are supplied with big collars, and pockets,and are sufficiently roomy to slip on easily over a tweed coat and skii t. MADGE.

London Polleunan Busy .in Warrington. Peter ComerforJ, Ship-yard, was charged at the Borough Police Court on Monday with having been drunk and disorderly in the Market Place, about six o'clock on Saturday evening, and also with assaulting P.C. Hughes while acting in the execution of his duty. He pleaded guilty. John Clifton, Lythgoes-lane, was also charged with assaulting the same constable. The Chief Constable stated that at about six o'cicck on Saturday evening, P.C. Hughes was on duty at Mat ket Gate, when his attention was called to a fight which was going on in Afarkebstreet. He went to separate those who were fighting, when Comerford rushed thrcugh the crowd and struck bun several times in the face. He also kicked him on the legs and in the back. Clifton also attacked the officer striking him on the back of the head and altogether treating hum very brutally. The constable could get no aid, until a constable of the Metropolitan Police, who was in the vicinity, came on the scene and assisted him. P.C. Hughes bore out this statement, and said if it bad not been for the assistance of the Metropolitan Police- man, ,he would have been very seriously assaulted.— P.C. Mealin, of the Metropolitan Police, also gave evidence.—The Chief Constable stated that Clifton had been once previously before the Court, and Comerford six times. He appealed to the Bench to deal with this case in such a manner as would act as a deterrent to others. It was no use putting small penalties upon these men, they were only an encouregement to others. —Comerford was sent to gaol for two months with haul labour.— P.C. Menlin was cordially thanked for his services by the Mayor and the Chief Constable.

IN BERLIN.

Germany is facing the leanest ( liristrnas on record. Shopping has seldom been done on so moderate a scale, and tradesmen in Berlin and other big towns are dis- consolate. Customers have been vastly fewer in number and have never bought so sparingly. The cLief sufferers arelradesmen who count on wealthy purcha: ers for disposal of jewels, furs, find motor-cars. But the great shops which rely on the multitude and the s;ieet hawkers are hardly better off. Ever) body who givcs at Christmas seems determined to retrench this 3ear, and the £1,000,000 ordinarily given as Christmas boxes in Lenin will probably diminish to half that figure.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

TII.74EAL SOCIETY WOMAN. At last 6 6 "ff hing like a true understanding of the society • woman has been arrived at, but it has taken a German journal to do it. Our Teutonic contemporary pays her a great compliment, for it points out that there is no other woman who could contrive as she does to be social, philanthropic, and domestic ali at once, with equal success and as a matter of course. She goes to her charity or poli- tical meetings, sells at bazaars, goes to balls, hunts, golfs, Niles, possibly drives her own motor, and, withal, is devoted to her babies, knows all the slang of her little schoolboy, can dress ker girlie's doll, and wash her own baby. Yet, comments "Miranda " in the Lady's Pictorial, we ourselves talk as if motherhood were dead in the land, and' as if our womenkind --were either hopelessly frivo- lous or wholly unsexed or insanely selfish. It is good to see ourselves as others see us; it helps us to get back our self-respect, and te realise that charity does not always begin at home.

A PRETTY PARTY FROCK.

Pictured in our illustration is a delightful little party frock of new and up-to-date shape, just the thing for Christmas and New Year festivities. The original of this charm- ing frock was carrio{l out in very soft ivory washing satin; but the same idea might be realised with equal success in washinr, velveteen or velvet, woollen-backed satin, or thick crêpe-de-chine. This little frock is cut square at the neck, and has a wide plastron front that rune from shoulder to hem, all the edges of which are finished with a band of

The Little skirt is gathered all round the waist on either side of the plastron, and is finished with four or five piping-3 at the bottom. The sleeves are simple, rather small, and of three- quarter length, and are finished with Ewe tucked cuffs. A dainty vest of tucked white Bxussels net fills the opening at the neck. INDIVIDUALITY IN COIFFURE.

There is no reason to &appose that women will not always put the graces of life in a foremost place in their interests, no matter how wide their sphere and responsible their working life may become. It is too clear, writes "Filomena." in the Illustrated • Lon- don News, that looks are of prime import- ance to our sex for the ' fact to be ever for- gotten. What will suit the individual may, perhaps, come to be more widely considered than ever before, as wfmen's intelligence • and, independence are brought more fully into play. Already we are far more indi- vidual, and styles are much less enforced upon the whole world of women by dress- makers, than was the case in Early Victorian days. There is always a prevailing fashion; but if a woman, for her own reasons, does not adopt it nobody is surprised. In the coiffure this assertion. of individual judgment is particularly apparent. The turned-back " Pompadour " is decidedly left behind by Fashion's ever-changing whims, vet thou- sands of women to whose faces it is suited continue, and will 'continue, to dress the hair in that way. This style is eminently becom- ing to certain types—the stately, calm coun- tenance on the one hand and the piquant irregular features at the other extreme. But there are a good many faces to which it is distinctly trying to turn the hair up high above the brow. The Greek coils and curls at the back of the head are naturally bring- ing back the flat dressing of the hair in front,

A SMART NEW EVENING COIFFURE.

The very smart and becoming coiffure p;e- tured in our sketch is an- excellent example of the latest ideas upon the subject of hair dress- ing for even;ng wear. It is simple, ladylike, and has a distinct style of its own. The hair is first waved in' wide natural-looking waves, and is then drawn from the front, back, and sides to the crown of the head, where it is • , • - ' .e. 44_ -• • • _ A • • disposed in soft, loose, and apparently care- less curls. Close to this knot of curls is placed, on the right side, a very smart knot of gold tissue, from which springs an elabo- rate white feather fantasy, spangled with touches of gold. The same idea is carried out in black with the greatest success, the knot being composed of heavily-jetted net, and the fantasy being of the softest black feathers thickly sprinkled Will sparkling jet.

CONCERNING LININGS.

Fashion, as far as skirts are concerned, is doing away with these for cloth and tweeds, declares the Queen, and many wear knicker- bockers of the same stuff beneath them. Some have silk petticoats fitting the hips like wax. This is a move on the side of economy, for silk linin,o6 never did wear well, and petti- coats of silk- have a longer life. Reception dresses mostly retain their linings; .this wise certainly in thin fabrics, or lines and ridges arise which are far from beautiful. We have done with ruffling and frou-frou; lin- ings nowadays are mostly made of soft silk.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

RISOTTO A L'ITALIENNIt.—Wash half pound of 4-ice, drain it thoroughly, and let it dry in front of the fire. Melt two ounces of butter in a stewpan, and when hot add a peeled and minced onion. Fry until of a light fawn colour. Then add the rice, and toss over the fire until the rice is slightly browned, adding salt and pepper; and by degrees, when the rice is coloured, a pint of stock. Continue to add a little more stock as the rice swells. When the rice is tender. stir lin a nreamast-cuptut of tomato sauce and two ounces of grated Parmesan cheese. Sprinkle a very little cayenne over the top, and serve at once,

PADGATE

CHRIST CHURCH PAROCHIAL TR-A..—The manna/ parochial tea and entertainment in connection with Christ Church, Padgate, took place on Tuesday evening and notwithstanding the Arctic weather, proved a great success. About one hundred sat down to an excellent. tea and no fewer than forty trays were given. Before the entertainment began, the Vicar (Rev. G. W.. Dickinson) said he was told that he was expected to give a resume of the year's work, but seeing that he had not a year's work to give a resulbe of, he thought he had better leave this task until be had been with the twelve months. He wished to thank the parishioners for the warm way they had received him from the first. He did not pretend for the moment that he was satisfied with the progress made, and probably in a year's time he would be able to say be was still dissatisfied, but they were going on better than he had expected and he had a great deal to thank them for. They had broken several records during the short time be had been here, notably in connection with the garden fete and the sale of work. He hoped the year to come would be one of happiness for all, and full of every blessing and that they would go forward with the work. (Applause.) To the musical programme, songs were tastefully contri- buted by Miss Elsie Litton and Mr. Humphreys: Miss Royston afforded considerable pleasure by her recita- tions • Mr. R. Scarisbrick amused everyone by his ventriloquial sketch, his playing of some novel instru- ments made by himself, and the recital of a stump speech ; while the dialogue "Our at home day," by Miss Heath,Miss Elsie Horridge,and Miss Lucy Harter, was also much enjoyed. Mr.H. J.lddon played two piano solos" Prelude in C sharp minor" (Rachnianinof f) and 2nd Mazurka (Godard) io good style. Mr.?. Blackwell discharged the duties of accompanist. The evening was fittingly wound up witita dance, Mr. W. Ainsworth supplying the music.

Carry a box of Peps in a handy pocket, and take a few at once whenever you begin sneezing. A sneeze is ward

If you are seized with a persistent tickling cough, Peps will rid you of the cause of the irritation, and will save you from an obstinate attack of sore throat or bronchitis. ===== 0002601_19090102_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
The 'Examiner, Saturday, January 2, 1.909.

1908.

THE STORY OF THE YEAR.

AT 1103 IE AND ABROAD.

The year 1908 has been one of change and upheaval. At home there have been dramatic Parliamentary situations; •abroad the pro- cesses have been all but revolutionary. This latter remark applies equally to the Near East, the Middle East, and the Far East. The year has seen " daring achievement in the scientific world. It has been the year of Inter- national conferences, religious, political, scientific, and commercial; the year of airship records and calamities; the year of industrial conflict; and the year of the suffragette.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BRITISH EMPIRE.

In the British Empire events have gene- rally made for peace, loyalty, and a good understanding. There has, however, been a dark cloud over India. Even the King's mes- sage on the fiftieth anniversary of the As- sumption of India by the Crown, while it was hailed with immense satisfaction by the great mass of the people, failed to mollify the discontented. It was found that an organisation existed in India for the perpe- tration of bomb outrages directed against British officials. There were some riots in Bombay, and it was thought advisable by the Indian Government to introduce bills dealing with explosives and the seditious press, and to pass them promptly. One of the most pain- ful features was that there were no fewer than four attempts upon the life of Sir Andrew Fraser, the retiring Lieutenant- Governor of Bengal. Lord Morley. 'made known that the disquietude would not retard him from advising the Indian Government to the extension of representative institutions in the near future.

South Africa has seen steady development. Mr. J. X. Merriman formed a Cabinet in Cape Colony upon Dr. Jameson's resigna- tion; but the meet tangible advance towards the realisation of South Africa's greater op- portunities has been the Convention at Dur- ban to bring about a South African Union. Canada has once more placed a Liberal Gov- ernment in power, with Sir Wilfrid Laurier at its head, and the British Postmaster- General has added to his laurels by arranging for a penny postage between Great Britain and America.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
KING EDWARD'S ENGAGEMENTS.

The King has continued his good offices es this country's greatest ambassador of peace— the work with which his name will be his- torically linked. He has met, upon the Conti- nent, the Czar, the Kaiser, the Emperor of Austria (who was celebrating his Golden Jubilee), and the rulers of Denmark, Norway, and Sweden. At home, his Majesty and the Queen received the Dowager-Empress of Russia, the King and Queen of Sweden, and the King and Queen of Norway. The King and Queen' of Spain also visited the Court, leaving at home the six weeks old Infanta. President FalHeves was welcomed on a round of gala and State ceremonial; and, with the King, he visited the Franco-British _Exhibition.

Yhe Prince and Prineces of Wales were feted by Berlin and Paris in March, and, in July the Prince of Wales sailed in the In- domitable to Canada for the Tercentenary Celebrations at Quebec, accompanied by Lord Dudley. From Quebec Lord Dudley proceeded to Australia to take up his new duties as Governor-General.

The King and Queen together, opened in July the new headquarters, at Charing Cress, of the Royal National Pension Fund for Nurses. His Majesty went to Leeds to open the New University Buildings and to Bristol for the opening of the new dock. After his usual visit to Marienbad, has Majesty opened the Queen Victoria Memorial School for Sons of Soldiers and Sailors at Dunblane ; and at Eton he opened a building in memory of the Etonians who fell in the South African War. The Prince and Princess of Wales opened the Rotherhithe Tunnel in June, and the Duke of Connaught assisted the fête for the veterans at Chelsea by his presence.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
CONGRESSES AND EXHIBITIONS.

The year is marked out pre-eminently as a year of International conferences and exhibi- tions. Provincial visitors by tens of thou- sands—French, Americans, and a sprinkling of Germans and other foreign nations— crowded, with Londoners the Franco-British Exhibition, the success of which was doubly assured by the Olympic Games opened in July by the King. The inauguration of the Exhibition by the Prince and Princess of Wales was preceded by the opening of the Scottish National Exhibition in Edinburgh by Prince Arthur of Connaught. The King opened the International Peace Congress. The Pan-Anglican Congress was followed by the Lambeth Conference of Bishops. The chief personal interest of the year in the Ecclesiastical world was the ap- pointment of the Bishop Suffragan of Step- ney, Dr. Lang, to the Archbishopric of York, on the resignation of Dr. Alaclagan. The Roman Catholic Eucharistic Congress ,in London attracted more notice than would otherwise have been the case by the proposal to carry the Host in the public. streets. Riot was threatened; the Prime Minister drew the Archbishop's attention to the illegality of this latter intention, and it was abandoned. The procession, under these circumstances, passed off peaceably. The International Congress of Religions took place at Oxford. There have also been International Conferences in London upon Free Trade, Art, Electricity and Moral Education. The Orient in London was opened in June. The Inter-Parliamentary Congress met in Berlin, and we, on our part, welcomed the German pastors and the German burgo- maSters. The International Naval Confer- ence on prize courts, promoted by The Hague Congress, was held in London in December. THE WORLD AT LARGE.

Quite at the beginning occurred the assas- sination of the King and Heir-Apparent of Portugal. France has had her war in Morocco; she has stood on her dignity with Germany over the Casa Blanca incident, and the recognition of Mulai Hafid's claim to supersede his brother the Sultan. In the Near East Bulgaria's ,assertion • of independ- ence and the annexation by Austria-Hungary of Bosnia and Herzegovina have not led to bloodshed, though it has been imminent for months. These - changes followed quickly the grant of a constitution to Turkey by the Sul; tan, a concession for which the Young Turks had been working for years. In the unrest Crate declared for union with Greece. 'Great Britain stood by the Young Turks in , their trouble, and insisted, with Russia, that the Treaty of Berlin could ,not be set aside with- out the agreement of the signatory Powers. Belgium has been consider:-.; how s'Ae shall take over the Congo from King Leopold with- out yielding extensive privileges of an undesir- able character to the concessionaires who had agreements under the, King. Germany has had two sensational trials— those of Herr Harden, whose four years' sen- tence for libel in the Court Camarilla ex- posure was quashed in May ; and of Count von Eulenberg, whose associations With the Emperor made the case particularly embar- rassing. Then in Russia there was the trial of General Stoessel, on whom was passed the death sentence (afterwards commuted) for suvendering Port Arthur. The question of a constitution led to blood- shed in Persia, the Shah bombarding the Par- liamentary builaings because of an ultima- tum against his absolutist methods. One pro- vince still holds out against him. The fear of Russian intervention brought two delegates to England to ask our good offices ,in urging on Russia the maintenance of neutrality.

China lost both Emperor and DONN nger- Empress by ckatli within a few hours. • The United States Presidential Election has maintained the Republican ascendency, Mr. 'raft succeeding Mr. Rcose.velt. America and Japan have come to an understanding to respect tl -z. integrity of China, the "open door," and cue another's interests in the Pacific.

THE KAISER AND HIS PARLIAMENT.

Perhan.s the most stirring incident of the year was me motseretion or the .Katser in granting an interview to an English journal- ist, and giving some extraordinarily frank

opinions, without considering Low those views would be regarded by his people. In this interview, the Kaiser (it was stated) made an energetic protest against the misre- presentation to which he had been subjected in England. He declared empill!elally that his heart was set upon peace, aira t.utt it was one of his dearest wishes to live on the best of terms with England. The portion of the disclosure that caused most stir was the as- sertion that the prevailing sentiment among large sections of the middle and lower classes of the German people was not friendly to England. Another matter which caused irritation in the Fatherland was the statement by the Kaiser that he had worked out what he considered to be the best plan of campaign for the British against the Boers at the time of the war, and had sent it to Windsor. Storm raged through the Ger- man Press, and in all the capitals, from Paris to Tokio, the Kaiser having made the suggestion that the main reason for the need of a strong German Navy was the awakening of China and Japan. Prince Billow met the Reichstag very soon afterwards, and a de- bate of extraordinary interest took place. In the end, the Chancellor said that the Emperor would, for the future, "im- pose upon himself, even in his private conversations, that reserve which was indis- pensable to a consistent -policy, and to the authority of the Crown." If that were not so" (said- the Chancellor) "neither I nor my successor could accept responsibility for it." THE SOCIAL YEAR: THE PEERAGE AND THE STAGE.

The social year at home has included a dozen weddings of note. Foremost among them the Marriage of Mr. Winston Churchill with Miss Clementine Hozier and of Mr. McKenna with Miss Pamela Jekyll drew' crowds to St. Margaret's. Besides the two Cabinet Ministers named . another Member of the Government--Mr. C. F. G. Masterman— married. The bride was Miss Lyttelton, and so two well-known political families were united. Then there were the weddings of three ladies of prominent name: Miss Gladys Vanderbilt with Count Szechenyi ; Miss Muriel Botha, sister of the Transvaal General, with R. C. Hawkin, Secretary of the Eighty Club; and Miss Jean Reid, daughter of the American , Ambassador, with the Hon. John Herbert Ward, whose wedding at the Chapel Royal the King and Queen attended. Lord Rosslyn, who was otherwise discussed for a " system " to break the bank," married in October an Irish lady, Miss V. M. Bayley. This was his third marriage. The Peerage continues to rob us of our Gaiety actresses. Earl Poulet married Miss Sylvia Storey, daughter of •Mr. Fred Storey, the comedian, and there were three .other Gaiety weddings, including that of Miss Marie Studholme with the son of a General. The other ladies of the Gaiety stage to wed were Miss Enid Leonhardt and Miss Elsie Kay. Miss Lena Ashwell married a West-End doctor later in the year. POLITICS AT -HOME. The Session has been Mr. Asquith's first as Premier. Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman's death (deplored by the whole nation) followed his resignation in little more than a fortnight. The Ministerial changes advanced Mr. Lloyd. George from the Presidency of the Board of Trade to • the Chancellorship of the Ex- chequer, and Mr. McKenna from the Presi- dency of the Board of Education to the office of First Lord of the Admiralty. Mr. Churchill entered the Cabinet as Present of the Board of Trade, and Mr. Runcir‘an be- came Education Minister; Lord Crewe, Colo- nial Secretary in place of Lord Elgin. Later in the year there were some further changes, Lord Ripon resigning the Office of .Lord Privy Seal, and Lord Tweedmouth giving up the Lord Presidency of the Council—on account of his health. Lord Wolverhampton (Sir Henry Fowler) was given the last - named office. Lord Fitzmaurice became Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster, resigning his post of Under-Secretary for Foreign Affairs, which was taken up by Mr. McKinnon Wood in the Commons. Other , noteworthy alterations, either at the beginning of Mr. Asquith's ad- ministration or later, were the advance of Dr. Macnamara to be Secretary to the Admiralty, -Mr. Edmund Robertson's enrolment as Lord Lochie of Gowrie, the appointment of Lord Lucas as Under - Secretary for War, and the entrance of Mr. C. F. G. Masterman into the Ministry as Secretary to the . Local Government Board. -

_ . At the beginning of the session, while Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman was still Prime Minister, the Scottish Small Landholders and the Scottish Valuation Bills, which the House of Lords threw out in the previous session, were sent up again and rejected. There was not much time before the Budget for con- siderable advance of important measures. But, thanks to the scheme of submission of Government bills automatically to four Grand Committees for their Committee stage, Commoners were kept actively em- ployed on matters of great moment. Mr. McKenna's Education Bill (the Government's second), was early introduced, but was held up because the reception offered very little hope of compromise on those lines. The Irish Uni- versities Bill was a triumph of Mr. Birrell'e statesmanship, inasmuch as it obtained ac- ceptance almost unanimously from both sides in both Houses, in the belief that tt would go a long way to settle the Catholic Univer- sity problem of Ireland. The Licensing Bill, the Miners' Eight Hours Bill, the Housing and Town Planning Bill, the Children's Bill, the Prevention of Crime Bill, the Pert of London Bill, and a number of other mea- sures made a heavy cargo, and between February and near the end of December the Houses got only six weeks' break for summer vacation. The Licensing Bill received its quietus at a meeting of Peers at Lord Lans- downe's house before even the second read- ing motion in the Lords wafe submitted. An additional seven years' security, beyond the fourteen years' time-limit, was not likely to save it; the Peers were not impressed by the desirability of so "regaining for the State un- lettered control of the licence." Local optipn was provided for at the end of the reduction period, with a big immediate reduction of the hours of Sunday trading, and .the nationalisa- tion of the compensation fund. Mr. Runciman's Education Bill, the Government's third, was introduced into the House of Commons at the end of November under auspicious circum- stances as being largely a measure of agree- ment; but difficulties arose, and it was drama- tically withdrawn under cireumstances which are fresh to readers. The Children's Bill ob- tained general approval, and the Prevention of Crime meesure was accepted with limita- tion of the indeterminate sentence proposal for special treatment of habitual criminals. The Housing and Town Planning Bill was withdrawn, owing to want of time, after mush laborious work in Committee; but the Prime Minister announced that it would be reintroduced and pushed forward early in the coining session. The Port of London Bill, with its scheme for the purchase of London Docks by agreement, was accepted by both parties. The Lords, after passing the second reading of the Miners' Bill, modified it in Committee.

Mr. Asquith' s Budget brought up the or- ernment's repayment of the National Debt to ;£47,000,000 ; the sugar duty was reduced by more than half, rind n: sum of £1,250,000 was. allocated to establishing an old-age pen- cion scheme. Lord Rosobery's Committee on the Re- form of the HOI/5,2 of Lords . presented its re- port, recommending that the number of Members should be reduced, by the plan of election which applics to Scottish and Irish Peers, and that other changes should be in- troduced to bring in eminent persons as life Peers, while keeping the total membership within 400.

At the complimentstry dinner to him at the National Liberal Club in December, Mr. Asquith spoke chiefly of - the rejection of Liberal measures by the Peers, and he said the principal issue of the- next election would be the veto of the House of Lords. THE YEAR OF WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE.

This has been a year of phenomenal and al- most mascices agitation in the cause of Women's Suffrage. The sole circumstance, however, of practical import has been the promise made by the Prime Minister and Mr. Lloyd-George that the Government will in- troduce an bleetollll Iklulfll But imme- diately before Parliament pes to the coun- try, to which it will be peesible to add a women's suffrage clause if the Housa so de- sires. The militant surfrag'sts have -.em- ployed the most cNtraordit:ary methods. Not

only have there been three separate attempts to " rush " the House of Commons, in Feb- ruary, June, and October, involving 120 arrests, but the House itself has been in- vaded. • A woman dashed through the glazed doors to the floor of the House wita the in- evitable cry for votes, and two others chained themselves to the grille and so addressed the Members from the Ladies' Gallery.. The, latter exploit led to the closing of the public galleries of the House. The most formidable demonstration was that in which the" suffra- gists joined hands . with the unemployed to nmet Parliament on its autumn reassembly, and some thousands of police were required to guard Palace Yard and the precincts of the Houses of Parliament. Arising out of .Lhe excitement of this time was the charge against Mrs. Drummond, Mrs. Pankhurst, and Miss Christabel Pankhurst, in connec- tion with which Mr. Lloyd-George and Mr. Herbert Gladstone were subpwnaed to Bow- street and cleverly cross-examined by Miss Pankhurst. A similar attempt, however, to call the Prime Minister and the Home Secre- tary to Leeds was defeated by a statement of the court, on application, that such evidence as they could give would not be useful to the defence. Cabinet Ministers have been in- terrupted at home by suffragettes upon the doorstep who refused to go away. Downing- street has lien raided, and the women even attempted to " rush " a Cabinet meeting on January 17th. They tried, without success, to present a p2tition when the King went in state to open Parliament. They helds two not- able demonstrations in Hyde Park ' and the Albert Hall in June. Under the Govern- ment's new municipal Act, the first Lady Mayor wt. elected this November—Mrs. Eliza- beth Garrett-Anderson, M.D., at Aldeburgh.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
INDUSTRY AND LABOUR.

The trade boom of the last year or two hav- ing spent itself, this year—industrially—has suffered some of the ill-effects of the reaction. Several strikes helped to swell the figures for unemployment, the principal being the strike of .the Lancashire cotton operatives and the strike of the East Coast engineers and ship- builders. The latter accentuated a tendency of modern times when Sir Christopher Fur- ness offered his men, on condition that strikes were ruled out in future, employees' shares, on deferred payment, carrying co-partnership in the undertaking. The offer was accepted, and the example may be followed elsewhere. Several working arrangements between rail- way companies to keep down expenses have been under discussion. The most important circumstance in connection with the represen- tations of Labour in the House of Commons has been the Court of Appeal's decision that trade unions have no legal title to make com- pulsory levies for the payment of members. It was decided to carry the appeal to the Lards. Mr. Henderson was elected leader of the Labour Party in the House of Commons. For unemployment the nation was better prepared this winter than sometimes ' in the past, thanks to agitation on the one hand and forethought in Mr. Burns's department on the other. The unemployed met outside Par- liament when • the House resumed for the autumn session, and a meeting of the L. C.C. was broken up. Mr. Victor Grayson provoked suspension from Parliament in order to draw attention to the subject, and there were several convictions for public speech which was a direct incitement to law-breaking. Mr. Asquith did three things to meet the evil: he increased the Central Unemployed grant, facilitated the provision of work by municipal loan, and antedated Government • undertak- ings, particularly in the dockyards. He pro- mised legislation at an early date on the whole problem. Mr. Burns persuaded local authorities so to time their public works that they offered occupation in the winter.

BIRTHDAY AND OTHER HONOURS.

Mr. W. P. Frith, the veteran painter of the Derby Day," was in January made a Commander of the Royal Victorian Order, and the freedom of the City of London was conferred in March • upon Miss Florence Nightingale. In the popular sense the prin- cipal of the Birthday Honours in July were the Peerages given to Sir Anthony Mac Donnell, Permanent Under-Secretary to the Lord-Lieutenant of Ireland, and to Mr. George Whiteley, until recently Chief Mini- sterial Whip; thg baroneteies to medicine and surgery in the persons of Sir Lauder Brunton and Mr. Cheyne; and the honours to learning and philanthropy—the O.M. for Dr. Henry Jackson, Regius Professor of Greek at Cambridge, the knighthood to Dr. Murray of Oxford; with the knighthoods' be- stowed upon Mr. J. J. Duveen (since de- ceased), who added the new wing to the Tate Gallery, and to Mr. H. P. Hartley, the gene- rous friend of the Primitive Methodist de- nomination. Mr. R. W. Perks received a baronetcy. In November Mr. A. it Wallace received the Order of Merit. THE Alba AND NAVY.

The year has given us, happily, no active service for the Army except a couple of,cam- paigns on the In_dian-Afghan frontier, and in these our arms were quickly and conclusively successful. The Army has seen transition at home. Mr. Haldane's Territorial scheme has been loyally carried out by the Lords-Lieu- tenant and the County Associations through- out the country. Unfortunately, the greater demands made upon the time of the men have somewhat reduced the 'numbers as compared with those previously serving in the Volun- teers. An important step has been the crea- tion of a Special R,_•serve of officers. Mr. Asquith has pleased all parties by his definition of the two-Power standard, • and the Government's programme of shipbuilding goes steadily along, as the experts like to say, Germany or no Germany.' Mrs. Asquith during the year has launched the sixth Dreadnought at Devonport, while other addi- tions to the first line have been H.M.S. Vin- cent and the cruiser Invincible. The year has contained several disasters during' man.- ceuvres. In the Channel the destroyer Tiger was cut in two by the cruiser Berwick, and thirty-six lives were lost; and in the night of April, 25th H.M.S. Gladiator, while _man- oeuvring without lights, was rammed by the American liner St. Paul off the Isle of Wight, when twenty-seven of the Gladiator's crew were drowned. LITERATURE AND THE DRAMA.

The three remarkable features of the pub- lishing year were. the issue of "The Letters of Queen Victoria" in popular form; the. sale of millions of copies of a volume of Queen Alexandra's photographs, which her Majesty graciously permitted to appear in the cause .of charity; and a cheap edition of Lord Morley!s "Life of Gladstone." Pageants have taken place at Chelsea, , Dover, Win- chester, and elsewhere; the Lord Mayor's Show included a literary pageant of the three centuries from Chaucer to Milton, and the, Milton tercentenary Was celebrated on a big scale at the beginning of December. The outstanding theatrical • events have been new plays by Mr. Pincro, Mr. J. M. Barrie. Mr. J. K. Jerome, Mr. Hall Caine, Mr. Gilbert Parker, Mr. H. A. Jones, Mr. Sutro, and Mr. Stephen Phillips.

SENSATIONS OF THE COURTS.

The mast dramatic episode in the civil courts was the award of £7,500 damages to Mr. John Murray, the publisher, in his action against the Times for criticism grounded on the assumption that tl.e Letters of Queen Victoria could have been produced much more cheaply than they were. Another case of heavy damages was that in which Messrs. Edward Cook and Co. were awarded £23,000 against the Amalgamated - Press in respect of what were called "the -Soap Libels." Mr. Lever had obtained £50,000 in the previous year, and it was understood • that the settlement of Messrs. • Watson's claim was for a similar sum. In November a lady named Mrs. Heath was aw arded £5OO in an action brought for face disfigurement against a lady "beauty specialist." A singular case of newspaper libel was that in which Mr. Artemus Jones, a barrister, obtained dam- ages amounting lo £1,750 from the pro- prietors of the Manchester Sunday Chronick in respect of a sketch of life at Dieppe, wherein was given his name as that of an imaginary suburban Londoner. An appeal was entered. The trial of Mr. Horatio Bottomley and three others charged with conspiracy and fraud in connection with two companies was opened in the first week in December.

PHOTOGRAPHIG NOTES.

By Veronica. FLASH-LIGHT PHOTOGRAPHY. At this time of year when there are no festive g ather- ingg, maliy opportunities occur for securing photographs I 'of People that are rarely together at other times of the year, but many of the most interesting events take place in the evening-time, n hen ordinary photography ls not available, and resort hss then to be made to artificial lighting. For those who have never attempted anything in this direction, let mu warn thorn, in photo- 'graphing groups, etc. to try to avoid as much as -possible, anyone looliin; directly towards the light. Another thing to guard against is that your flush of light does not pe,netrate the lens. That is best guirded against by having a rather long tube or hood fixed over the lens, bat no not make this tubs too small in diameter, otherwise you may cut oft some of the field Of view.

VARIETIES OF LIGHT. There are many different means of utilising magnesium light, one of the simplest being the fltsh Tapers which are thin sheets of collodion imprognatel with magnesium powder. Then there are the flash- light candles, Gek3 !' and" Flashaxe," which can be bought in various sizes to burn in time varying from two seconds to thirty seconds. Again there are the numerous magnesium lamps on the market. One of the best that I hare seen recently is known as tho " Dago," and I have recently seen a most excellent Ontograph of a debating society called the flatlvell (Jill ['arils- Inent. that was tfiken by this flashlight, in tihich the 'consumption of powder was only fifteen gr 01S. Mortiner's hook '"gnesiwn Light Photograo'ly," in which he deals With diffaront phases of this class of work.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

The Parish Church.

Special Christmas services were held at the Warring- ton Parish Church on Sunday, and were largely attended. In the morning, the preacher was the Rev. A. J. Brat, and in the afternoon the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) delivered an address to young people. In the evening, choral evensong was held, when selections from "The Messiah" were given in praiseworthy style by the choir. Mr. F. M. Darby presided at the organ.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
Evangelistic Mission.

Another evangelistic service was held in the Bold- street Wesleyan Schoolroom on Sunday evening at the close of the ordinary service,Mr.Dean presided and sang a sacred solo in good style. Two Christmae carols were sung by five boys (Thomas Gartland, Gerald Doncaster, Leonard D.-mcaster,Reginald Parry and Charles Hamblett) entitled" Once in royal David's city " and "0 come all ye faithful." An address was given by the ftev. T. Miller from the two passages. " Unto you is born this day a Saviour " and " He shall save His people from their sins." Master John White officiated at the organ.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
" Waymarks."

At the Baptist Church-,fitfolborne-street on Sunday evening the Rev. R. E. Letheren took for his text : Jeremiah xxxi.2l., "Set thee up waymarks." The sermon was devoted to a review of the year from three stand- plias suggested by the three texts in the Old Testa- ment in which the slate word is used that is repre- sented by aVaymarks " In 2 Kings xxiii. 17 the word refers to a title or inscription to mark the place where something is buried. Within a few days, the preacher said, the year will have gone down into the grave of what we call the detdpast. Its moments with their mercies, its doings with their duties, its hours with the opportunities they held, its weeks with all their worries, its months with their mingled meanings of gluipess and grief, will soon be buried with —lilt a date to mark where they lie. Re went on to speak of the duties undone, the-opport tunities negleCted, of how the influence and the memory of the day may still haunt us like restless and unhappy ghosts and that only in the Gospel of Christ was the secret told that laid the ghosts of the dosd past and sweetened its memories and redeemed its influence. In a second passage the word was used to mark that cr Wet' ought to be buried and the preacher spoke of those things that lie above the field of human life corrupting and defiling it. In the passage from which the text was taken the word referred to something that ought to be bourne in mind for the better guidanee of those that come after and our own wiser use of the days to come. Among the way narks of the year Ir. Letheren referred to the spirit of revolution and unrest in the farther and nearer East, and the- " moral strength " the changing situation calls for ; he spoke of the legislation of the year, and said that half a million old people would remember it through through their remaining dtys as the year that brought them Old Age Pensions to make their life's eventide easier and brighter. lie regretted, speaking for him- self, the failure in the compromise of the Education controversy; and with regard to the action of the House of Lords on the Licensing Bill he had not any words to characterize it that were directed to that time and place. lie felt that they were on the eve of a campaign which would put their christianity to the severest fst ; that the issue was between the sovereignt of Christ in human affairs, aud the shameful reign of secularism and selfishness.

We have received from the Free Trade Union, 8, Victoria•street, Westminster, S.W., a copy of their new monthly, " The Free Trader," issued .at a penny. It is an ably-written journal, which ought to be in the hands of every opponent of Protection. for it is an armoury of facts in itself against the great fiscal heresy. The exiliination • of Professor Marshall's famous memorandum is very well (Lane, and so, too, is an open letter to Mr. Bomar Law. The Union also send us a pamphlet, "Ucemployment and Tariff Reform," which shows conclusively that neither Free Trade nor Tariff Reform can put an end to either unemployment or fluctuations in Trade. The leaflets include reprints of Mr. Chamberlain's Free Trade speeches, reply to the argument that the Patents' Act is Protection ; Mr. Harold Cox's article on "Protection and Unemploy- ment," reproduced from the " Westminster Gazette," and others showing the effects Of Free Trade upon various industries.

jßobert Caldwell, one of the witnesses from ' the United States (who had gone home quickly), could not be extradited on account of illness. Mrs. Robinson, charged with perjury, made a full confession, and was sentenced to four years' penal servitude; and Mrs. Mar- garet Hamilton, in her seventy-eighth year, was given eighteen months' hard labour. At the instance of the Local Government Board, various municipal scandals were in- vestigated at the Old Bailey. In May, James Calcutt, a building contractor to the Mile End Guardians, was given r,ix months' im- prisonment, and he was subsequently a wit- ness in charges against ten members and ex- members of the Mile End Board, and against seven members and ex-members of the Pop- lar and Stepney Sick Asylums Board. Im- prisonment followed in every case. Two members of the Joel, family provided, ftS prosecutors, three sensations. Franz von Veltheim was • sentenced to twenty years' penal servitude for blackmailing Mr. Solly Joel. Mr. Robert Sievier, arrested at San- down Park races on a charge of threatening to publish defamatory matter in the Winning Past concerning Mr. J. B. Joel in order to extort £5,000, was acquitted. The verdict was popular, vast crowds cheering Mr. Sievier as Lie left the Old Bailey. Two Manor Park fur- riers indicted for threatening to murder Mr. J. B. Joel were sentenced to penal servitude, one for five years, the other for three. Of murder cases the great outstanding cause—that of the charge against Thaw=---was at last concluded. This second trial ended in Thaw being found insane and sent to an asylum. In England public attention was held in a peculiar degree by the murder of Mrs. Luard (wife of General Luard) in a wood near Sevenoaks. This was followed, some weeks afterwards, by the suicide of the General, who left a letter saying that he was harassed beyond endurance by anonymous Letters. In France, the profoundest sensation in this category has been the mystery sur- rounding the death of the artist, M. Steinheil. His wife made charges which sub- sequently appeared to be unfounded, and the interrogation of, herself by the judicial authorities caused much excitement. Another mysterious Crime in the West End of London was that of the. murder of a young Polish Jewess in Bernard-street, Bloomsbury. Early in the year a young soldier named Maguire was indicted for the murder of a lady whose body was found on one of the cliffs at Bourne- mouth, but the police abandoned the prosecution. In November, in a scuffle with a man who had attempted to snatch some banknotes from the counter, Mr. Schlitte, a partner in a foreign bank in Shaftesbury-avenue, was fatally stabbed; other men were either wounded or shot before the assailant could be zaptured. Several robberies were marked by more than ordinary audacity. There was a succession of raids on banks. The most in- genious was that of a young man who, calling himself Mr. D. S. Windle, visited a number of' branch banks in the south and south-west of London, and secured about £2,320 by means of cheques. Shortly before the Franco- British Exhibition closed two ingots of platinum, valued at £l,OOO, were stolen from a show-case.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
CASUALTIES AND DISASTERS.

The world's tale of casualties and disaster* need be told only briefly. In the United States, Chelsea, a suburb of Boston, was de- stroyed by fire. The financial loss was esti- mated at £2,000,000 sterling. The town of Chilapa, in Mexico, was swallowed up by earthquake. In September the Bahamas were swept by hurricane, and several vessel* off the islands were wrecked. The floods in Hyderabad in the same month involved appal- ling loss of life. A few weeks earlier at Stamboul 20,000 people were rendered home- less by fire. In November between 300 and 400 miners perished in a fire in a Westphalian mine. Other colliery disasters include the entombment of ten men in the Hampstead mine, near Birmingham; ten deaths in Nor- ton Hill Colliery, Somerset; seventy-six in the Abram Colliery, near Wigan; and four- teen at Washington, near Durham. Two un- usual accidents with fatal results were the balloon explosion at Shepherd's Bush, 4 result- ing in the death of a lady secretary and work- man; and the panic at a cinematograph en- tertainment at Barnsley, sixteen being killed and many injured. One other extraordinary accident must be chronicled: In November a lady artiste shot dead het assistant while he was playing the part of "William Tell" at the Middlesex Music Hall. The death toll 11 the sea does not appear to have been quite so heavy as in some years. Perhaps the most terrible event was the sink- ing of a Japanese cruiser by an explosion off the Pescadores Islands, nb fewer thart 160 officers and men being drowned. From fire on the steamer Scandinavia off Malta eighty- three persons, mostly Mussulman pilgrims, lost their lives. In cheering contrast to these heartrending events should be mentioned the splendidly serene lamding of all the passen- gers and crew of the pleasure steamship ArgO- naut, which collided in the English Channel in a fog. The number of persons thus all safely brought to shore was 240. The boat sank soon afterwards. HISTORIC YEAR OF SPORT.

In Many, respects the year is notable in the history or sport. All branches were stimu- lated by the Olympic Games held in London, when over 2,000 athletes, representing twenty countries, competed keenly for national honour. As was to be expected, despite American ascendency at prior festivals, the British Isles secured first place, though, es- pecially in athletics, American competition was formidable. Dorando confirmed his vir- tual win -over the American, Hayes, in the Marathon Race by beating him over the same distance in America in November. All ex- cept one of the Olympic records were equalled or broken. Apart from the Games the achievements of Hammond and Wills are the most remarkable athletic events of the year. T. E. Hammond broke all world's records by walking 131 miles 580 yards in twenty-four hours. Wills, at Munich, paced by a motor cycle' pedalled sixty-one miles 973 yards in one hour. The Derby brought a great sur- prise to sportsmen. Chevalier Ginistrelli's Signorinetta won by two lengths at 100 to 1, and a few days later confirmed her ability by winning the Oaks. The Football Cup was won by Wolver- hampton Wanderers. The cricket season was not marked by any special event. Yorkshire were easily Champions, and did not lose a single county match. James Braid, with the lowest (and best) record . score secured the Golf Championship for the fourth time. The King's Prize at Bisley again went to Scot- land; Private Gray, of the Scottish Rifles, was first with a record score of '325 points. Cambridgg on the boatrace in very good style. Interest in sculling was revived after much neglect in this country by a match in the Thames for the championship of England. Ernest Barry, of Putney, beat George Towns, of Australia, a former world's champion, in record time. _ _

Some extraordinary motor speeds have been recorded at I3rooklands, chiefly on cars driven by A. Newton. The year has seen consider- able advance in aerial navigation. Wilbur Wright has made some notable flights in France; hie brother Orville, in America, amid sueee66-es, came to grief while carrying a passenger under Government test. The pas- senger was killed. Count Zeppelin's big dirigible balloon broke from its mooring and was destroyed near Stuttgart, and in the In- ternational race a number of balloons ended in the North Sea. FAMOUS PEOPLE DEAD.

The death-roll of the year contains many notable names. The principal are: The Grand Duke Alexis of Russia, ex-President Cleveland, the Duke of Devonshire, the Earl of Derby, Lord , Overtoun, the Marquess of Linlithgow, Lord Glenesk, Sir J. Lawson Walton (Attorney-General), Sir J. C. Mathew (ex-Lord Justice of Appeal), Sir J. C. Day (retired judge), General Linievitch (com- manding the Russian forces during the latter part of the Russo-Japanese war), Marquess Nodzu (the famous Japanese General). General IS idler, Father Ignatius, Dr. Edward Caird (late Master of Balliol), Canon Flem- ing, Mr. Ira Sankey (the evangelist), the Rev. Benjamin Waugh, founder of the N.S.P.C.C., " Ouida," M. Sardou, " Uncle Remus," Sara sate, Miss Lily Hanbury, Sir Henry Drum- Wolff, Sir Nicholas 0' Conor, Sir Howard Vincent, M.P., Colonel Kenyon Slaney, M.P., Sir W. The weakest resolution for THE NEW YEAR the New Year is that sug- AND ITS gested by the Irish parody PROSPECTS. humorist—to make no,

resolutions. It is true that divisions of time are mere devices of convenience and that most of our great outstanding decisions of life must in the very nature of things be deter- mined quite apart from the aid of the calendar. But the idea which is really behind the thought of the welcome feesh start with the beginning of a New Year has its value, and does not need the apologies which are usually offered for it when good resolutions are talked of. No man is so con- ceited on January ht as to think that he has made the best possible use of the twelve months which have just closed. He may be better for the reflection that with the opening of another period he will gain by the experi- ence of the past. At any rate, the sentiment will not harm him if he applies it in examina- nation of himself, good-humouredly, but none the less definitely on that account. In that connec- tion the propinquity of New Year's Day to the Christmas festivity is happy because it tends to cause the inquiry to be conducted in a spirit of kindliness and sweet reasonableness. The genius of our own race is against violent change, and the feeling of the present age is certainly in harmony with the lessons of nature—that development of any kind is best achieved gradually. Therefore, such resolutions as we may make on New Year's Day in this part of the twentieth century are likely to be tempered by moderation and avoid- ance of extravagance of thought or deed. The outlook for the year is one calculated to test the individual, as well as the nation, for os individuals we are bound up nationally, and the two considerations, though they may have many differences, cannot be separated. The present year is entered upon, from the national point of view, with less optimism than narked the corn- mencernent of the last. The national mood just now is to regard things rather gloomily but we hardly think that pessimism is justified. We are at peace with all the world, and our relations with foreign Powers were never more friendly; we have paid a large sum off the National Debt ; the war taxation was by the last Budget still further reduced ; more than half a million of the most deserving aged poor to-day experience for the first time the joy of long-talked-of pensions ; and money .is cheap for commercial enterprise—for strengthening our commerce by judicious specula- tion. So all classes have a great deal to be grateful for. Toe New Year is a swum ens to new enterprise, and on no other condition is there salt and savour in its sentiments. It is to be a year of great activities in many realms. Of polities nothing need be said here, except that great issues will be Industrially the outlook is brighter. One hopes that,at any rate,ten and a half million work- ! ing days will not be lost in this country during 1909,as in 1903,because of trade disputes. For the Churches, too, the year will be a busy one, and after recent happenings, it is devoutly to be wished that in every walk of life the year may begin and continue in that disposition of charity which is the great human key to all its good fortune. Toe dark daye are over and done with ; our faces are now to the light. We are returning to the sun. Almost before we have tinae to lift our eyes from the task the snowdrop and the violet will be gemming the bank side and the underwood in the coppice. The birds' song will sound a fuller note in welcome 1 of each returning day, and the day will prolong ' its primrose twilight in every evening. The world ' has begun the serious business of another year, and there is profound parable in the fact that its task will be accomplished by an ordered progrss which is irresistible and unremitting.

If the present Government PENSIONS AT LAST. had performed no other legislative achievement than to pass the Old Age Pensions Act which came into force yesterday it would have done enough to earn the undying gratitude of the democracy. Pensions for the aged and honest who have fallen behind in the keen competitive struggle for exist- ence have been promised by statesmen and Governments for a longer time than one cares to recollect. They never appeared likely to materialise until the Government of to-day took office and declared its determination to translate promise into fulfilment. This Government lisd the power, it had the will—an i what is more important —it had the heart. Yesterday-pensions were paid over the Government counter, and the dream of the poor and toilworn was realiEed. Tile Act, as the " Manchester Guardian " so aptly says, "begins the humanisation of our system of poor relief which . . we hope will continue." There is now a distinction, wkiich has long been lacking, between poor relief and Poor Lariil , relief. The latter pauperises, and the former uplifts. In course of time the newer, and more humanitarian method, may supersede the older. We trust that it will. Already, a time limit has been placed on the present disqualification for pensions which bears so hardly on those unfortunate people who have been compelled to accept relief undsr the Poor Laws. In 1911 even they may shake off the shackles and participate in the Government bounty. Eventually there will be no disqualification for which a would-be pensioner is not personally responsible and blameworthy. i This is what should be. At present the means available compel a certain amount of restriction but there are 600,000 old people eligible already for the Government grant. Jealous Conservatives

try to spoil the picture by saying that the six and a half millions required will not be forthcoming. They should remember that taxation is much less now than when their party was in office. During the last ten years taxation has increased by one- tenth; the taxable resources of the country have increased by nine-tenths, so-that the Chancellor of the Exchequer is a long way from the end of his tether.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Congratulations to the Apollo Prize Choir on winning the first prize at Chester Christmas Eisteddfod for the third time! * * *

Councillor Peacock is making hay while the sun shines, and not allowing any concern about the election petition to cramp his energies as the present representative of Orford Ward. Having already brought the bowling green scheme to the fore, he is now tackling the price of gas to users of slot meters, and will move for a reduction at the meeting of the Council on Tuesday next. * *

It will be some time before the circus at Market Gate is complete. Improvements do not follow one another with delirious rapidity, and there is an obvious pause in this cparter. The shop lately tenanted by Mr. T. J. Lee bas been let again at £l2O per annum. Though threatened it seems likely to live long.
