* * *

It has been suggested to the Sanitary Works Com- mittee that the night-soil vans should be fitted with rubber tyres, but the Committee have vetoed the suggestion on the ground of expense. These dis- turbers of the silent watches of the night have always struck us as being more noisome than noisy. If the odours they exhale so lavishly could be ru')ber- tyred they might get awe" (Flicker.

Ifessina, now destroyed by earthquake and sea, was founded by pirates in 732 B.e. It has its interesting associations with both English history and English literature, for Richard Coeur-de-Lion spent six months there on his way to the crusades, and the scene of "Much Ado about Nothing" is laid there. Bolntia bombarded it for several days in 1843, and it was the last place in Sicily to be taken by Garibaldi in 1810. Earthquakes have often visited it disastrously in the past. When Goethe landed at Messina in 1737 ho found it still a ruined city, demolished by the earth- quake of 173. * * *

Some interesting figures were given at a meeting of the Leeds Free Church Council recently as to the proportion of Free Church prisoners in prison. Alluding to the representations made to the Some Office with regard to the visits of Nonconformist ministers to prisons, the report, we gather from the "Free Church Chronicle," stated that out of a total prison population of 20,136, there were only 253 Wesleyans, eighty-four Baptists, sixty-six Methodists, sixty-nine Presbyterians,tlairty-four Congregationalists, font- Salvationists, three Bible Christians, Oro Plymouth Brethren, and one Quaker. * * *

Cold as the last few days have been, they have been almost torrid compared with the closing week of 1860. At nine o'clock on Christmas morning of that year the temperature in Hyde Park was 17deg. below freezing-point. At Beeston, near Nottingham, accord- ing to the "Times," "the temperature at the same time was Bdeg below zero at four feet above the ground ; and, on the grass, 13.8 deg. below zero, or 45.8 deg. of frost." During the previous night, from seven p.m. to eleven p.m., the temperature never rose as high as zero. In the last week of December, 1793, the mercury fell in London to 16deg. below zero.

A good story is told in the "Lady's Realm" of Mr. Radvard Kipling. Recently he was a guest at a literary -dinner at which, besides several leading authors, were a great many men who knew something about literature and tried to make up for what they lacked by a good deal of pretence. Trying to hold Kipling's attention, one of these started a discussion concerning the spelling and pronunciation of the English language. " Have you ever noticed, Mr. Kipling," he said, "that in the entire English language there are only two words beginning with su 'that are pronounced as though beginning with sh ? Those two are sumach and sugar. Having made an exhaustive study of the subject you may take it from me that this is so." Bored though he was, Kipling's politeness did not desert him. Assuming an expression of interest, although his eyes twinkled behind his glasses,he quietly asked, "Are you sure?" 4, * *

The Watch Committee is apparently in favour of instituting a new kind of distinction for the men in blue to strive for. In the minutes of the Committee for the last meeting are entries awarding P.C. Green a suit of plain clothes and a rise in wages from 265. to 28s. per week for apprehending Frederick Hallows for shopbreaking ; and P.C. Newey a reward of £1 for apprehending William Tomkinson for the same class of offence. We do not, in the least, object to this recognition of smartness. Policeman do many things in the public service for which they get no special thanks or reward. Now the capture of a shop breaker is a substantial achievement and merits recognition. We hope other officers will be spurred to emulate the deeds of P.C.'s Green and Newey. If no shopbreakers are available, a burglar eliTald do as well and command the same figure. Jndging from reports from the suburbs there are plenty of burglars waiting to be laid by the heels. Up and at 'em bobbies !

* * . * A pensions officer in the course of his inquiries in a village between Bury and Bolton was interrogating an old lady—et lifelong resident of the village. Bearing in mind the disqualification that residence in a foreign country may sometimes prove to be, he asked the old lady whether she had ever been abroad. The applicant considered the question for a moment and then replied in the negative. The officer noticed her hesitation and in a warning tone said : "You had better be careful, madam ; the penalties for wrong statements may be very heavy." The old lady trembled. " Well, I have been abroad, now I come to think of it," she said. " Oh, you have been," said the officer ' • " Then why couldn't you say so b efore then. Really, madam, I must warn you again to be careful. Where have you been ?" " Well, now," said the old lady, with manifest concern: " I didn't think it mattered all that. Ify memory isn't as good as it used to be, but I mind I was at Blackpool twice and at Bolton three times." If Mr. Stead's dictum as to the supremacy of Irish orators is possibly open to question, there is certainly no denying their pre-eminence in the production of bulls. Even the stately Burke himself has been credited with a characteristic example, while more modern instances have been numerous enough. Was it not Mr. Field, of Dublin, who convulsed the House by remarking, " The right hon. gentleman shakes his head—and lam sorry to hear it"? Another of his fellow-countrymen not long ago objected to " intro- &lcing fresh matter already decided," while a third declared that the Government was "iron-bound with red-tape." Mr. Gladstone used to say that the m-st eloquent speaker he ever hef.rd in the House of Commons was Richard lialor Sheil—though he admitted in reply to someone who asked if Shed did not scream a good deal that the indictment was true. " Sir" replied the G.0.M., "he was all scream." The late Lord Salisbury, on the other hand, regarded John Bright as the greatest orator of modern times. " I have met men who have beard Pitt and Fox," Lord Salisbury once said "and in whose judgment the eloquence of those famous orators was altogether inferior to the finest efforts of John Bright."

The Postmaster-General is showing consid•rable willingness to introduce reforms in his Department , which will fill a long-felt want. The latest is a new kind of paroel post which has been established by the Post Office. It is known as the "insured box system," and it will be operative between the United Kingdom and France and Belgium. The new service is , intended to provide for the carriage of jewellery, precious stones, articles of gold and silver, and so on. The weight of the insured box must not exceed 21bs., nor can it be more than 12 inches in length. The Postage on an insured box is one shilling, and the insurance fee is 4d. for the first £l2 insured and 2d. for every additional £l2 up to the maximum of £4OO. The lack of such a scheme in the past has been severely felt. Small parcels containing articles of great value have had to be sent to France without the protection of registration, and without hope of recom- pense if they were lost. The consequence has been that most of the jewellery passing between the two countries has gone by messengers, or has been brought over by travellers at the request of friends for postage here.

Presentation to ex-Police Inspector Smith.

Yesterday afternoon at the Police Station ex-Inspector Samuel Smith, who recently retired on pension from the local Force, after 26 years service, was presented by the Chief Constable, on behalf of the members of the Force, with a handsome eight-day striking clock, suitably inscribed, for himself and a gold brooch for Mrs. Smith.—The articles were supplied by Messrs. Eustance.

The twentieth Chester Christmas Eisteddfod was held on Boxing-day, and attracted several hundreds of people, there being crowded audiences both in the afternoon and evening. The following six choirs sang in the male voice contest (test, "The S3n of God," by Jenkins) : —Brymbo, Mr. R. J. Roberts; Cambria (Newtown, Montgomery), Mr. J. R. o.Evans; Packtuoor, ltr. James Smith ; Tunstall Male Glee Union, Mr. W. A. Lonsdale ; Warrington Apollo, Mr. IL Berry; and Wigan Harmonic, Mr. A. Bullock. The adjudicator said that the competition had been ruined by the canker worm of bad intonation, for which generally the inside parts had been account- able. There had been a close contest tstween the Warrington and the Wigan choirs, but he had no hesitation in awarding the prize to Warrington for what was on the whole a very good performance.

COAGL7LINE," KLINX," TENASITINE.' Cements for mending all things.—Advt. Penny Popular Concerts. Saturday evening's concert in Bold-street School- room was one of the most attractive yet .held and there was a crowded audience, a number being unable to gain admittance. The Rev. T. Miller presided, and during an interval in the programme delivered a short address. Miss Ruth Cocking, of Manchester, and Mr. Andrews, of Liverpool, rendered several songs in a highly creditable manner. Miss Ethel Kent is a clever child pianist, and in addition to playing a couple of pianoforte solos, she acted as accompanist throughout the evening. gr. Stewart, of Manchester, supplied a capital ventriloquial sketch, and Mr. G. Barratt, of London, kept the audience in a state of merriment with his humorous songs. Another popular feature was the smart exhibition given by a perform- ing dog trained by Mr. Edwin Nield. BRASS AND COPPEIR HBARTR SWITES, see window display for new and artistic patterns.—J. S. GARLAND, 51-53, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Presentation to the Rector. After the early celebration at the Parish Church on Christmas morning some thirty or forty ladies and gentlemen, representing a larger body of parishioners, called at the Rectory and the Rector's warden (Mr. J. S. Craik), after wishing the Rector and Mrs. Willis a very. happy Christmas, said the deputation wished to give them a Christmas box from a number of parishioners, in accordance with the custom said that Christmas was a fitting time to bestow presents upon those they loved. gr. Samuel Taylor, the people's waiden. then handed to Canon Willis a large and handsomely framed photograph of himself taken by Mr. Birtles. In making the presentation Mr. Taylor spoke of the respect with which they regarded the Rector and Mrs. Willis, and expressed hopes as to their future welfare and happiness. The Rector, who was as deeply affected as he was completely surprised, thanked the parishioners heartily for their kindness and wished them, too, "A very happy and blessed Christmas."

Fire at _Messrs. Rylands Brothers' Works.

Some alarm was caused shortly before two o'clock on Sunday afternoon when it became known that a fire had broken out in what is known as the stretching room, over the offices of Messrs. Rylands Bros.' Church-street. Fortunately however, the outbreak was so promptly dealt with that it never assumed serious dimensions. The works bell was rung and the foremen and others assembled and got to work with the firm's fire appliances. A telephonic message was despatched t the Fire Station, and Superintendant Turner and the Deputy Superintendent, with a full complement of men and two engines were quickly on the scene. It was seen, however, that the fire was in hand and as little water was used as possible to prevent further damage being done to the offices below. A. large hole had been burnt in the floor and a portion of the beams destroyed. The damage is covered by insurance. The cause of the outbreak is unknown.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOGIETY. Concert in Parr Hall.

The miscellaneous concert given by the Warrington Musical Society on Boxing night was excellent in pre- senting a most interesting programme, but it curiously failed to attract the full attendance which might have been expected on a night so largely given up to enter- tainments of all kinds by the public. But, though the audience was not strong in point of numbers,the room was nicely filled and bore a cheerful aspect, the dis- crimination and enthusiasm shown in regard to the music providing an animated light and shade in the evening's proceedings. The most important item was Elgar's " The Banner of St. George "—quite a big work for the society's members when the total amount of choral singing embodied in it is considered. Having regard to the present unbalanced state Of the chorus the forceful and dramatic descriptiveness of the piece was splendidly brought out, and, in speaking of the singing, one scarcely knows whether to admire most the subtleties of Elgar in his simpler moods as in the voicing of the "wail of anguish" of the Sylene women or his stern and strenuous ruggedness in the com- plexities of the conflict with the dragon as may be instanced at the occurrence of the words "the knight of Cappadocia dauntless stands," in the male voice parts. But there was no mistaking the fact that the society's members had got firmly hold of the spirit and intention of the work,vvith the result that the story was told in the music with conviction and great earnestness. The endings of the two scenes comprised in "The Banner" are beautifully contrasted, the evasive yet entrancing subject associated with the words " 0 beauteous Love ! " being in the first scene tenderly and expressively rendered by the soprano and contralto voices amongst whom it is most con- spicuously heard, while the moving march theme of the epilogue was a culminating effort on the part of all concerned which was worthy of .all praise and evidenced the thoroughness of rehearsal and the perfect control the conductor, Mr. Crossley, had over his forces. While the orchestral parts to Elgar's work were exceptionally well played, agiinst t'uis due acknowledgement should be placed the plain fact that the executive capacity of the violin section was largely overstrained in the Zarnpa overture and in the last. of the three Henry VIR dances (Gar man). An interesting feature was the appearance of Miss Clarice Newall at the pianoforte in Mendelssohn's G minor concerto in which she greatly distinguished herself, her playing of the onerous solo part being marked by a delicacy of touch, a reserve of power, and it steadiness under trying circumstances which are as remarkable in so young a performer, as they are both admirable and indicative of genuine ability. A. most enthusiastic eccore was given to Miss .Newall to which she responded with a charming rendering of a "Study" by Loesclihorn in which facile gracefulness was again a most noticeable quality in her technique. As a solo violinist Mr. Arthur Catterall came to Warrington with the highest credentials, and his performances of the Mx Brach concerto, though somewhat marred by the faulty orchestral playing in the finale, was a masterly performance. His tone, as heard here arid in the Polonaise (Vieuxtempa) and the Romance in G- (naethoren) is magnificent, while the ever-changing mood of the Polonaise and the exigencies of the double- stopping in the Romance were difficulties in the surmounting of which Mr. Catterall alike gloried and triumphed. Mr. Horatio Connell sang some baritone songs of no special interest in quite a satisfactory manner, and the choral members of the society further rendered Gounod's " 0 Sing to God "and a number of Christmas Carols with a breadth, a degree of taste and a tone volume which left little to be desired.

MR. F. STOTT IN NEW ZEALAND. THE CONDITIONS OF LABOUR.- EMPLOYMENT SCARCE.

{To ME EDITOR OF TETE " EXAMINER-91 NOVEMBER iGTa, 1908.

Sir,—l arrived at Auckland on October 19th, after • a long and dreary voyage. For seven weeks I did • not set foot on land, and for over four weeks we saw neither land nor a passing vessel. Daring September we held a meeting in the dining-room, when I gave an address on Socialism to a large and appreciative -audience. We expected to have plenty of opposition lout were surprised to find that not even a single question was asked. Socialism was the main topic for discussion by the passengers for several days after • the meeting. It was interesting to hear the various reasons which were given as the cause which had led many of them to emigrate. Unemployment at home appeared to be one of the principal causes. It was my intention to post a letter for the Examiner" from Auckland, but owing to a severe strain of my back I was practically a cripple during our stay at that port and correspondence had to be somewhat neglected. Having had no news fr -)m leaving Liverpool, it was a real pleasure to be able to peruse a newspaper upon our arrival in the Dominion, and the news of interest to me was that there was a "cotton "strike in existence at home, that Victor Grayson had been suspended for the Session, and that the General Election in New Zealand was to take on November 17th. Perhaps the most important news to myself was to find that there was a trade depression here, and consequently a large number of unemployed. "The speeches of the candidates for Parliament are very strange reading to a Britisher. There is no mention of Education or Licensing Bills which are being so keenly discussed in the old country, nor are there any unemployed demonstrations, although there is a large army of unemployed men in the Dominion. One has only to take a walk during the daytime in the vicinity of the wharf here in Wellington to be assured of the fact that there is a number of men out of work. I saw a report in one of the Wellington newspapers to the effect that there were at least one 'hundred carpenters out of work in the city, and this is during the time of the year when the building trades are usually busiest. -What the conditions will be in the winter season one does not care to imagine. It might appear to b 3 unjust for a " new chum " to attempt to comment upon the conditions of labour SO soon after arrival in the Dominion, but I have been here quite long enough to realize that New Zealand is not a place where employment is so easily obtained "we had been led to believe before leaving home. I found upon my arrival in Wellington that there were quiet a number of engineers unemployed and that it was very difficult to obtain a situation. The bulk of the engineering shops are mall and give employment to very few men. It was a new and not very pleasant experience for me to search for work, for I had fortunately never been out of employ- ment before. For some nine or ten days I tramped about from one works to another. At last I was 'fortunate to obtain temporary employment and made a start last Tuesday. Two of my colleagues who came on the "Surrey" have not vet been able to obtain a situation, and one of them has a wife and four children dependent npon him. A few days after our arrival 'here one of the Shaw Sarin steamships from London brought between five and six hundred passengers, and one could not help but feel sorry for many of them who had an impression that employment would be obtained almost immediately upon arrival. There is a strong and growing feeling against so many immigrants bcing encouraged to come here, and the policy of the Government in granting assisted Passages, is being severely condemaed by many of the Parliamentary candidates, and also by a large section of the public. It has surprised me to find how many now corners there are who regret having, 'left the old country and there are many here who would be glad to get back home again There are many, of course, who have been more fortunate and are much better off than in the old country. Adverting to politics, I find that in one of the Wellington constituencies there are seven candidates for one vacancy. There is a Labour candidate and also a Socialist candidate among them. Some of the candidates deliver addresses of two to three hours in length. It is expected that the Government (Liberal) will be returned with a slightly decreased majority. In many of the constituencies the interest taken in the Local Option vote far exceeds the question of the election of the Member. The electors are asked to vote on the following questions : (1) I vote that the licencea- in this district be discontinued. • (2) I vote that the licences in this district be reduced. (3) I vote that no new licences in this district be granted. The publicans here as at home are advising the -people how to vote, and asking them, in the interact of temperance, to vote for continuance. Tinge placards are exhibited from every hotel with instructions how to vote. A large increase in the No licence "vote is expected. Next week I will send you a contribution com- Menting on the result of the election.—Yours truly, FRED STOTT.

THE NEW YEAR. How it was Ushered in at Warrington.

The birth of the year 1909 was heralded in Warrington by the ringing of Church bells 9.n d the wierd sounds of works " buzzers." Although the night was raw and the streets slushy, large crowds seemed to find amusement in parading about until midnight and then proceeding to let the new ! year in" after the approved fashion. Watch-night 'services were held at many places of worship and were largely attended. New rear's Day was practically a general holiday and there were big gatherings at the football match and the theatres.

A Treat to Poor Children.

There was an animated scene in Bewsey-street, yesterday afternoon, the occasion being the New Year's treat to poor children organized by the Wycliffe Women's afternoon class, conducted by Mrs. Armitage. Wycliffe Hall was besieged long before the time announced for the tea—half-past five—by about GOO of the poorest children in the town. Each child on entering the hall was presented with a bag containing bread and butter, currant and seed bread, and a mince pie. Tea was served out to them when all were seated. An excellent entertainment followed. An action song and dance by Miss Alaybury's class was loudly applauded. A magic lantern greatly pleased the children, and was manipulated by Mr. Hill, assisted by Mr. Osborne. Miss Mason's class gave a dialogue, which was much enjoyed. Songs were also given by Messrs. Bennett and Kenyon. Vociferous cheers were given for the subscribers whose kindness had made the treat possible. Oranges were distributed to the children as they departed for home. Mrs. Molyneux and Mrs. Hewitt ably discharged the duties of hon. treasurer and hon. secretary respectively. WATCH-NIGHT SERVICES.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH.

The usual watch-night serviee was held at Wycliffe Church on Thursday night. There was a good attend- ance and the Rev. 11. Wall preached. In the course of his brief remarks he impressed upon his hearers the duty of surveying the past year in the spirit of thank- fulness. It was tr time when men looked back and when their thoughts turned to the year through _which they had passed. If they looked carefully they would find that though there might be some degree of bitter- ness and disappointment it was not all labour and tears. There was more of sweetness than bitterness, and more of joy than sorrow. There was that in every life which should call forth the deepest gratitude, if they only realized bow God had been lending and directing them, and if their survey the past year called forth their gratitude they would be able to face the coming year with a larger hope and a greater courage believing that He who had led them thus far would guide them to the end.—The last moments of the Old Year and the first moments of the New Year were spent in silent prayer, after which the congregation joined in singing For- ward be our Watchword.' Mr. Wall then asked for the prayers of those present for one of their members who was passing through the pain of bereavement, and the meeting closed with the Benediction. BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

There was a good attendance at the watch-night service at Bold-street "God's Guidance" (Deuteronomy viii. 2) was the subject of the Rev. T. Miller's address. The book of Deuteronomy had exercised a profound influence upon the religions development of the Jews, "and its teaching," said Dr. Ityle, " loads more directly than that of any other Old Testament book to the revelation God so loved the world that He gave His only begotten Sen." The Law-giver appealed to the memory of the nation ; history was shown to be a revelation of God's purposes. Spiritual impressions lost their lustre through the influence of sin. Memory must he awakened. A good memory was a manifesta- tion of personal character ; forgetfulness suggested ingratitude. It was well to reflect upon God's dealings with us. Richard Jefferies in "Wild Life in a Southern County" described the joys of bird and animal life, using the bleached skull of a hare, which he had picked up in a furrow, as a text. It was with similar feelings they should use the solemn service that. night so suggestive of death, as an opportunity of reviewing life's joys. The uncertainty of life was symbolised in the strange movement of the pillar of fire and the cloud. These natural manifestations were apparently erratic in their changes. Sometimes the cloud rested for a whole night, at other times the march had to be resumed in the dead of night, and the carefully formed encampment was hastily abandoned. A few days, a week, a month or a whole year might be spent in one place so great was the uneertAinity of the Divine direction. In our lives there was a similar incalculable element. Our strength was to have our confidence in Divine wisdom. We were not forgotten. As Silas Marner said "There's dealings wi' us." God's guidance varied in its method. In the Wilderness a fire encircled by clouds was the agent; at Jordan it was simply an ark. There was no monotony in the Divine govern- ment. By these mechods our characters were tested and perfected. Our ignorance and helplessness were brought home to us by our limitations. The result of all the changes in life was a will in harmony with the Divine will.

===== 0002601_19090102_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
BEWSEY-ROAD WESLEYAN CfIIIRCII.

The watch-night service at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church was conducted by the resident minister, Rev. E. B. Keeling. There was a good congregation, and the service was hearty and enjoyable. The sermon from the texts Genesis xli.,sland Oeuteronomy viii.. 2-5, dealt with the fact that the painful and distressing experiences are forgotten when the real victory of life is won, or when its most inspiring Work is undertaken. St. Paul in his eager search forgot the things behind, and ever pressed forward to the greater things to come. But the guidance and training, the education and chastening of men by their God, are things to be care- fully noted and remembered. "The ways of God to men " are justified when the end is reached. When this is understood, the particular tools that may have been used in the process, cease to be of very much import- ance, and may he safely forgotten. When the new heaven and the new earth come to be, the former things s‘all not be remembered nor come into mind. GOLBORNE-STREET BAPTIST CIIURCII.

A very interesting series of gatherings was held at Golborne-street Baptitt Church on New Year's Eve. A Service of Song entitled "The Coming of the Angels" was given by the choir under the leadership of Mr. Dawson. Mr. Henry Roberts presided over the meeting and gave the connective readings. his rendering, combining both sympathy and skill. Responding to a vote of thanks which was carried with the greatest heartiness he spoke a few words of encouragement to the people at Golborne-street in the great work they were undertaking and wished them the largest measure of success ; adding that if he could help them in any way he would be pleased to do so.—At nine o'clock a hot supper was laid in the school- room, all the preparations and arrangements being carried out by a Committee of men, lines being imposed upon ladies who were discovered in the least act of assistance. Fairly large numbers sat down and the proceedings were evidently enjoyed.—At eleven a watch night service was held, being conducted by the Rev. R. E. Letberen who gave an address on Mark xi. 4. " FATHER CiIRISTM S "AT THE INFIRMARY. " Father Christmas "visited the Infirmary on Boxing ' Day and gladdened the hearts of the patients by pro- sentin-g them with seasonable gifts. The benevolent old gentleman was impersonated by Alderman Smethurst, who was replendent in a rod robe trimmed with fur and also assumed the white beard always associated in the juvenile mind with Santa Claus. It was obvious that he gloried in his task of helping to make the day a mernorible one for the inmates of the institution. Those who have not actually seen the Christmas decorations at the Infirmary eon have little idea of the beautiful effect produced and it is no exaggeration to say that some of the wards, so often the scene of suffering, looked like a veritable fairyland with their artistic embellishments. Saturday's pro- ceedings took place in the large unused ward at the top of the building, in the presence of a large gathering. The most prominent object, which attracted the admiration of the youngsters in particular, was a large Christmas tree, gaily decorated and brilliantly illuminated with electric light. NOTES ON GAMES AN3 ATHLETICS. Rugby Football.

By " Ovalist."

Congratulations to the Warrington men on their dashing wind-up to the old ye.ar.,- Right through the first half of the season they had been labouring under the handicap of a bad start in league matches, and it is only with their last engagement that they have raised their percentage beyond the half-way line. The substantial victory over Leigh on Christmas Day caused 50 per cent to be reached for the first time, and the succeeding triumph at Widnes took Warring- ton to 53.12. At least they are in the top half of the League table, and with a powerful team available they should make further substantial progress.

"The advance by Warrington was well maintained yesterday, when the Wirepullers inaugurated the New Year with a handsome victory over their neighbours from Runcorn. The Linnets had evidently come determined t) wipe out the defeat at the hands of Warrington at the end of November, but the task proved too much for them. Warrington have seldom had a harder struggle for victory, however, and it was only superior goal-kicking which won them the victory. Runcorn scored first and were always causing the home men anxiety right up to the final tootle of the whistle.

The absence of Brooks made a great difference to the home side's effectiveness. Lloyd played well as substitute, but he has not, of course, got the dash of Brooks and does not get the ball away to the three- quarters as quickly. The latter did not get many chances in consequence, and they were well spotted by the visitors. The men in green played their own kick and rush game to perfection, and the speed of the front rank caused the Warrington defence a lot of anxiety. Tilley was sorely troubled in the first half ,but he stuck to his guns and responded finely in the closing stages. Taylor was the hero of the Warrington team. He played a great game, and not only made the opening for Fish singlehanded, but beat the Runcorn defence beautifully in the second half and scored under the posts. Fish did an immense amount of work, and kicked the three goals which won the game with all the confidence and accuracy of his palmiest days.

This defeat for the Linnets somewhlt spoils their chance of getting into the first four. They were not disgraced however, and gave a display which would have beaten most teams. The tendency to roughness by the Runcorn forwards marrcd their play. Flannagan and Unsworth played a fine game at three- quarter,and the irrepressible J,lly was everywhere, as usual. Padbury's speed was a greater factor in the game,and the Warrington men could take no liberties.

To-day, the Ebbw Vale men are due at Warrington, and should provide the Wirepullers with an easier victory. The fact that they halved the points in the first engagement gives a spice of interest to the match.

Quite a sensation has been caused locally by the transfer of Dan Isherwood, the old kVarrington captain, to Leigh. The latter will soon have half a team of Warrington men,though so far they have not made any captures which are likely to cause grief in the home camp. Most people thought that Isherwood had retired, but there should be some useful service in him yet. And Leigh are hard hit by injuries. Isherwood has done nothing for Warrington since his benefit match, so that his departure can hardly be considered a loss to the playing strength of the club. His only game this season was against Leigh in the opening match, and he did not distinguish himself then. Warrington men will wish the popular threequarter a Happy New Tear with his fresh club mates.

A correspondent who saw England beat Wales on Monday sends me the following: " One does not take these games seriously and none hold this idea, apparently, more strongly than the Northern Union Committee. For the losers Foley played a magnifi- cent game, being here, there and everywhere, and was undoubtedly the best forward on the field. The Welsh half-backs were poor. The three-quarters were con- siderably hampered by the bad passes of the halves, but Jenkins was often doing dangerous things for the opposition, but even his runs lacked their usual Sash. Paddison was a terrible failure. For the winners Gifford was incomparable, with Brooks a good second. Hilton was fair to moderate, knocking on several times. Tyson was not seen to advantage, and Batten was not a tower of strength. He did one or two smart things but certainly did not play up to his reputation. Of the home forwards Mann was the one who filled the picture, always being handy when there was any

scoring going on.

It was at half and centre where the home lot were most beaten, Jenkins playing brilliantly at the base of the Warring- ton pack and keeping the third line in motion. Fish made a couple of electrifying runs, and O'Neill had the pleasure of scoring his first try for Warrington. On two previous occasions—once on Christmas Day— lie has crossed the rut only to be whistled back. The tall Welshman is now displaying real International form in the loose.

CHRISTMASTIDE.

HOW THE FESTIVAL WAS

I CELEBRATED AT WV:WIGTON. General expectations of a ran Christmas were belied at the eleventh hour. A cold south east i wind on Christmas Eve was suceeedad by a sharp • frost aad when Chrism-as Day dawned the conditions were as seasonable as could be desired. The air was exhilaratirig and a welcome spell of sunshine during tha morning and early afternoon added to the pleasure of being out of doors. Services of an appropriately ! festal character were held at numerous places of worship in the morning, and the Anglican and Roman Catholic Churches had been brightly decorated for the occasion with flowers and evergreens. At the various public institutions every effort was put forth to make the day a happy one. In gaily decirated rooms seasonable fare was served and even the poorest of the population were able to eater into the spirit of the joyous anniversary to some degree. Christmas Day is, of course, the great home festival and the family reunions round the home fireside never lose their charm. Everywhere there were evidences of festivity and fie true Christmas spirit found full expression in kindly consideration for the needs of those in the poorer walks of life. For football enthusiasts the great attraction was the match at Wilderspool between Warrington and Leigh and a large crowd witnessed the encounter. : Boxing Day was cold but bright and the foJtball matches at Warrington and Widnes were well attended. In the evening there were large audiences at the theatres, where capital holiday programmes were submitted, and the Musical Society's concert in the Parr Hall provided a musical treat which was liighly appreciated by a fairly numerous gathering. Sunday witnessed the first fall of snow this winter. By ton o'clock flakes were descending and soon the ground was covered with a beautiful mantle of white, thus putting what everyone thought was the finishing touch to the "old-fashioned" Christmastide, so often pictured in the imagination but, in these modern days, very rarely experienced. But there was more to come. During Monday night and early on Tuesday morning a blizzard almost unprecedented in its fury swept over the country and Warrington bad its full share of the visitation. The wind travelled at a tremendous velocity and the fine powdery snow which fell for hours was blown into • huge drifts. Many people had to cut their way through ! banks of snow before they could leave their houses. The tram service was suspended and residents at Stockton Heath, Sankey Bridges, Winwick-road, and • Padgate lane had perforce to walk to town in a bhindin snew storm. Shortly after one o'clock it ever, thanks to the exertions of the officials MP'the Tramways Department, the service was resumed as usual except that only one line was used to Stockton Heath and that the speed was somewhat slower than usual on all the routes. The storm was a blessing in disguise to the unemployed, for it was the means of providing work for about 200 of them in clearing away the snow. Railway traffic was disorganized to some extent, many trains from a distance arriving much behind the scheduled time. AT TfIE POST OFFICE._ •

I As in previous years the work of the Post 0:11ce during the Christmas season was very heavy, there being approximately an increase of 30 per cent. in letters, and j 12 per cent. in parcels. Owing to Christmas Day falling on Friday, the pressure was spread over the first four days of the week, Wednesday and Thursday beinr, very busy days. The resources of the Post Office were taxed to their utmost in successfully dealing with the vast volume of work. Heavy posting of Christmas missives (those not included in the "Advance Posting ") commenced very early on Wednesday morning /3rd December and continued up to the last oollectionl at night. Although two clsys iu advance of Christmas Day this heavy posting seemed to indicate that a large section of the public were complying with the request of the Department to post early, which is a sign that the public may eventually bring themselves into line with the Advance Posting Scheme. Notwithstanding that the postings of Wednesday were so heavy as to many times exceed those of a normal day, due in a measure to the fact that the various firms in town were apparently "clearing up" their correspond- ence, the postings of Thursday were far in , excess of those of Wednesday. From as early aa 8 a.m. until the last Collection on Thursday the 24th, missives of all kinds continued to pour in without cessation or appreciable diminution, and required all the eftorts of the ordinary staff supplemented by numerous outside helpers, who had been specially tmined for the purpose for some time before,to sueoessfully cope with the pres- sure,and it is satisfactory to know that the vast mass of correspondence was all cleared away by the night mails. As already mentioned in the " Examiner " the town letter boxes and sub-office letter boxes were closed at 6 p.m., and the head office box at .0.30 p.m. on Thursday, for out-going mails, but although freely advertised both in this manner and by other methods, several thousand missives were posted too late for night mail and could not, therefore, go forward until the next, day. As in previous years the system of " Advance Posting " was resorted to, the Post Office undertaking to receive letters for delivery on Christmas Day from the 14th to the 21st inclusive, but again an adequate response was not made by the public, as the number so posted was only 24,436 and when it is realized that approximately upwards of 2,500,000 missives were dealt with during the brief period of the pressure, and that a normal average day's posting is 25,000 it will be seen that the result was very dis- appointing as regards the advance posting. Although the now head office is considerably larger than the ol one, there was not, and never is, at any post office,roonf' to deal with all the Christmas work,missives and parcels in one building, and it was necessary therefore to requisition the old office for the pareel work. There is a tendency on the part of the public to make up parcels very loosely and in flimsy covers, so that not a little time was spent "in doctoring"—that is securely repacking them up, before they could be despatched to their destinations. The outdoor portion of the work was greatly facilitated by the use of motor cars for the collection of letters from town boxes, these cars proving of great value where speed is required. Horse vehicles for the heavier work of collecting and delivering parcels were in use as usual, while mail carts and motor vehicles plied between distant Sob-offices, and as far even as Liverpool, Manchester, Wigan and Chester for heavy traffic, tkat is to say parcels work. Judging by the enormous amount of work dealt with at the Post Office; the popularity of Christmas greetings has not by any means commenced to wane, but, on the contrary, it continues lo increase in the ratio given. DETAILS OF THE FESTIVITIES.

At the Workhouse the usual dinner of roast beef, vegetables and plum pudding was provided in the large dining hall which had been elaborately decorated. The Lla7or and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Forshaw) visited the institution and were Recorded a hearty welcome. In the course of a short address the Mayor wished the inmates the compliments of the season and assured them that he would do his best during his term of office to find employment for those who needed it. He hoped that when they began to build the new bridge it would find employment for a great number of people. If the inmates were poor they could be good, and as long as they were good they might be sure that others who were in a better position would endeavour to make them happy. In the evening a Christmas tree for the children and a smoking concert for the adults enabled all to spend a happy time and the Christmas festivities of 1933 will long be recalled with pleasure. At the Drill Hall there was the usual distribution of hot-pot, bread and mince pies kindly provided by the , Mayor and Mayoress. A Warrington Centenarian.

Miss Mary Barton, who resides with her piece Mn. Buiton, at 244, Ilanchester-road, celebrated her one hundredth birthday on Saturday and was the recipient of many hearty congratulations on the happy event. Miss Burton was born in Kendal and at the age of nineteen went to America and, lived there for eight years.o n returning to England she lived an active life at several places in turn, and in October, 1893, when she was 87 years of age, she came to Warrington. Considering her weight of years, Miss Burton keeps wonderfully well, but latterly her eyesight has been failing and she is now unable to read. She is a very cultured lady and a most interesting c,onversationalist.

At Widnes on Thursday, Airs. LAMP TRAGEDY AT RUNCORN. SORDID STORY.

An extraordinary story was revealed at an inquest, held by Mr. T. Ridgway, coroner, at Runcorn on Tues- day evening. The victim was Annie Grindley, aged twenty-six years, single, living with her parents in Surrey-street. She died in the Cottage Hospital on Sunday night from burns caused by a paraffiu lamp explosion three weeks ago. Elizabeth Grindley, the mother, deposed that her daughter had been keeping company three years with a young man named William Whitlow. The girl never said a word against her sweetheart, but said that she was in trouble, and that he was " going to have her." Witness understood that he was going to marry her. Witness told her daughter that something would have to `.,e done, as she was keeping one child, five years old, by another man. On Saturday night, the 12th inst., Annie went into Whitlow's house, which was next door. She was sober, but more jolly than usual, as see was going to make the children a kissing bush. Once before she tried to commit suicide when she was led astray. After her daughter had entered Whitlow's house witness heard a row, and went to fetch her home. Ant,i. said, " No, I'll have my revenge for what they have raid to me." Witness understood her to mean that Whitlow had said he would not marry her. The girl picked up the lamp, and witness said " Oh, Annie, don't ! Come home." The next thing witness saw was her daughter iu flames. She did not see the lamp thrown. They all helped to put out the fire. Witness said that her daughter was a sober girl, but bad once been in court for being drunk and disorderly. Witness was reluctant to answer some questions, and the coroner told her she was trying to hide something. William Whitlow, chemical labourer, said that he had kept company with Annie Grindley for three years. When she entered his parents' house on December 12th she and he had a few words. The girl said, " Will you marry me ? " and he replied, " No, I shan't," and then the accident occurred. lie had said that many a time to her. They had bad many a quarrel in friendship. The Coroner : Funny to quarrel in Friendship. What did she do ?—She picked up the lamp to her shoulder. She threw the lamp, and the stand hit me on the top of the left ear, fled the lamp went back on her and exploded, and a drop of oil came on me. She got the " wick part." A drop splashed on my head and set my hair on fire. I did not get seriously hurt. Grindley's mother put her jacket over me and put out the fire. I went to the tap and put water over my head. The Coroner : As soon as she caught fire you ran away ?—Yes, there were three more in the house. [lad you been drinking that afternoon ?—I had been drinking a drop, not much. Was she sober ?—I don't know. I didn't see her more than five minutes. She was in a fit of tamper. I know that somebody must have been" narking " her before she came into the house.

The Coroner : I suppose you think it is a clever thing to keep company with a girl and then get her into trouble ?—No.

Are you ashamed of it ?—T might have married her later on.

Sarah Whitlow, mother of the last witness, said that her son and the girl were talking so quietly that she took no notice until she thought the lamp had gone out, and she was going to get a light when she saw the girl on fire. Annie Vra`i not quite sober. The Groner : It is an extraordinary thing that Grindley's mother could hear the row next door, while you sat there quietly, not hearing any row.—lt was an argument between themselves. Peter IVhitlow, the father, gave evidence of a similar character to last witness, and Dr. Brunton described the severe nature of the burns sustained by the girl. She was in great agoey, and said " It is all through Mrs. Whitlow." Ile thought she meant that Mrs. Whitlow was against the marriage. Ho thought the lamp had exploded. 'The Coroner said that the evidence was most unsatis- factory. Ile believed that the witnesses had arranged what to say, and were not telling the whole truth. It appeared certain that th,se two had quarelled, and that the young woman took up the lamp and an explosion oecured. The behaviour of several of the witnesses was most callous and reprehensible. • The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death," and asked the coroner to censure the witnesses, which he did.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

NEW ClanWEI AT HAYDOCI.

Lord Newton on Saturday afternoon laid the founda- tion-stone of the new church of St. Mark at Ilaydock. It is to cost £4,500 of which £3,800 has been raised, and there witl be sitting accommodation for 350 people. The Bishop of Liverpool was present. EARLESTOWN IN DARKNESS. EXPLOSION AT THE GASWORKS.

For three hours on Thursday morning, ass result of a destructive fire at the Nowton-iu-Makerfleld Council gasworks, Earlestown and Newton were plunged in darkness and unable to get gas. It appears that about six o'clock an iron tap connected 'with, the governor through which the gas pssses, and which had become frozen, suddenly burst, and as the water wi.s allowed to run unchecked the gas escaped and became ignited by contract with some gas-jets in the vicinity. In a few minutes the governor house was in flames, and before the fire could ibe got under the whole building was gutted,whilst a jet photometor was utterly demolished. It was nine o'clock before a fresh connection could be made cud the supply of gas to the town continued as usual.—The accident was fur-reaching in its conse- quences, for the inability to get gas either for lighting or motive-power forced several of the local works to remain idle for a time. A terrific and disastrous explosion was narrowly averted.

THE NEWTON DIVISION.

CONSERVATIVE SEARCII FOR A CANDIDATE

Lord Newton on Saturday night opened a Conserva- tive workingmen's club at Blackbrook, near St. Helens, and in addressing the members afterwards, he referred to the selection of a Conservative candidate for the division. He looked upon this as no light task. One or two names had already been submitted to them, but however urgent might be the need for a candidate he would impress upon them that it was perhaps wiser not to be in too great a hurry. He did not think they ought to jump at the first man who offered to stand as their candidate. He certainly thought it would not matter very much who stood next time because he felt absolutely certain that almost anybody could turn out their present representative.— (Laughter and applause.) Judged by the success which attended the Government candidates at the present time the chances of their present member being re-elected were as far off as anything that human ingenuity could conceive. As a matter of fact it was not as easy to obtain a suitable representative for a county division as some people supposed. Some people had the idea that being a member of Parliament or the Parliamentary candidate for a county division repre- sented the summit of earthly happiness. To represent a constituency in Parliament was no doubt an honour- able thing, but it made a very considerable demand upon a man's power and time, and constituencies were very much more exacting than they were in the far-off days when he (Lord Newton) first offered himself as a Parliamentary candidate for that division. It would be no use to palm off an incapable man ou the constituency. Regarding the position of Liberalism in the country, Lord Newton said everybody knew that when the next. election came there would be a tremendous reaction against the one-sided system of Free Trade, and there would be great pressure brought to introduce tariff reform at the earliest possible opportunity. He thought it extremely unwise to delude people by telling them that when tariff reform came into force distress and unemployment would at once vanish. He was rather disposed to question whether depression and unemploy- ment were, in reality, more %cute now than they were

some years ago, or whether it was not that the public !conscience WAS more awakened to these evils. We had no doubt reached a state in which we ' found it impossible to tolerate a system under which large numbers were unable to find decent means of subsistence.—(Elear, hear.) This really showed an advance in public morality, and he thought it was a very welcome sign that greater attention was paid to questions of this kind, and that serious faorts were being made to combat the terrible evils which unfortunately still prevailed. They knew what the Government proposals for the unemployed were. For his part he was not prepared to find any serious fault with them, but according to the Govern- ment's own confession these so-called remedies were not remedies at all, but only pilliativesrand would only have a temporary effect. If they were going to grapple with unemployment and its attendant evils they must begin by making a radical alteration in the country's fiscal policy. It might be said by some persons that a simple course would be to tax peraons like himself and bleed them quite dry—(laughter)—but it would make no appreciable difference throughout the country.

ST. HELENS.

FOUND WITH A FOWL. Patrick Kennedy, a labourer, of 93, Tainworth-street, was remanded at St. Helens on Tuesday on a charge of fowl-stealing. Police-constable O'Donnell was in Chancery-lane during the height of the snowstorm, about half-past two in the morning, when he saw Kennedy sheltering in a pxskage. They had a conversa- tion, but the officer noticed the fowl under the man's jacket and locken him up.—At the police court, Mr. 'l'. lialublett was the only magistrate that could be found, and Kennedy was brought before His Worship, and r4manded until to-day.

.A GILDED FARTHINQ. Thomas Mullen, a young collier, of Eldon-street, wa charged at St. Helens, on Monday, with stealing 103 from Joseph Herrick, the landlord of the Liverpool Inn Thomas Appleton, the barman, said that on Saturday afternoon Mullen had a drink with a companion in the public-house and tendered a coin, ahich the barman thought was a half-sovereign, lie gave Mallen 9s. 9d. change. The men stayers in the house nearly three hours. Mullen accused the barman of having gives him short change, stating that it was a sovereigu that he had given. Appleton said he thought it was half a sovereign, but he had put it on the sideboard, and he would look at it, lie then found that it was a Laded farthing. ila'deu was fined 40a. or twenty-eight days. THE BLIZZARD. The snowstorn completely paralysed the St. Helens tramway service on Tuesday, and made it very difficult for people to get aboat the district. The fall had been several inches thick, but tha greatest difficulty was the drifts. The high wind had driven the snow into huge banks, which blocked the roads at different points, arid the residents of houses whose doors at all faced the wind woke to find that the snow had been driven inside the houses' where it lay several feet thick. The surveyor's department was early astir, and all the nnemployed who preeented themselves were given work at assisting to load the snow into carts, by means of which the main streets were cleared as quickly as possible. Altogether, about 150 Corporation workmen and unemployed were put on this work, but a great many more unemployed would have been taken on if they had presented themselves. The tramway officials ' found it impossible to deal with all their sections to Prescot, Itainhill, St. Helens Junction, Haydock, Denton' Green, Parr and %%India City, and the whole morning waft spent in salting and endeavouring to clear kfew of the lines. Sr. HELENS POULTRY SHOW. The first annual open show of the St. Helens, Proscot, and District Poultry, Pigeon, and Fur Association was held on Saturday, at the Y.XI.C.A. Gymnasium. There was an excellent entry of close on 350, the judging being in the hands of Mews. P. Proud (Birkdale), %V. M`Nelly and E. 11. Cooper. The show was opened by Mr. S. Robinson, the president, who congratulated the • chairman, Mr. Burdekin, on the capital entry, and said that shows like that no doubt attracted the notice of outsiders and proves to them that there was something more pleasing in the town than ugly works and waste heaps. NICE DISHES.

CWELEE OMELET.—Potatoes boiled, one pound; cheese, half a pound; one egg; butter, one ounce; mustard, pepper, and salt to taste. Mix those ingredients thoroughly, using a little milk to assist the combination; then place the mix- ture in a buttered dish, and bake until a light brown. It should be noted that cheese, although in itself fuln flavoured, requires the assistance of considerable seasoning when used with other substances in cookery. SCALLOPED TOMATOES.—Ta.ke five large toma- toes, perfectly ripe; -scald and peel them. Cover the bottom of a deep dish thickly with ,butber. Then put in a layer of tomatoes, seasoned slightly with a little salt and cayenne and pow- dered mace or nutmeg. Cover them with se layer of breadcrumbs and butter, then another layer of tomatoes, and proceed thus till the dish is full, finishing with breaderumbs, and laying a few pieces of butter on the top. Some prefer the addition of a little raw sugar. HONEY CASES OR COOKIBI3.—Two cupfuls of koney, one cupful of butter, four eggs, one Cup- itil of butter-milk, one good quart of flour, and one level teaspoonful of carbonate of soda. If too thin, shake in more flour. tse thick howl' and the same cup for measuring the various in- gredients. Be sure to mix the honey, butter, and eggs well together. It can be made richer if desired by using cream instead of butter-milk. Bake in a slow oven, as it burns easily. To make small cakes, use a little more flour, so that they will roll out well without sticking to the board. Add flour as desired. This reoipo else makes a rood gingerbread.

Suspicious Characters at Warrington. At Warrington Police Court, on Tuesday morning, James Towey was prosecuted under the Prevention of Crimes Act, having been convicted twice in seven years, with being found in the dwelling-houses of 58 and 21, Allen-street, for an unlawful purpose. —The Chief Constable said that about half-past six on the night of December 18 prisoner was found in one of the houses and handed over to Police-constable Berry. He had a very bad record, having been previously convicted for housebreaking and burglary. A. few weeks ago prisoner came to the Police Station and saw him (the Chief Constable) and the result of his visit was that a situation was found for him at a local. works. However, he soon gave up the place and returned to "the old game. "There was not one redeeming feature in his character.—The magistrates , sent prisoner to gaol for six months.—Michael Moran, a stranger, was sent to gaol for a month for being found on the enclosed premises of William k'urnival, smallware dealer, Bewsey-road, on December 19 About eleven o'clock on the night in question prisoner was found lying on some straw in the stable pretend- ing to be asleep. Three panes of glass had bees broken in order to gain an entrance into the stable.

POSTAL AND SAVIN63 BANK RaIuLITIONS

For further particulars see "Post Office Guide," at any Vost Office. POSTAGE STAMPS. Books containing 12 penny and 23 halfpenny stamps are now on sale at each post office. Price 2s. INLAND CORRESPONDENCE.

The term "Inland Correspondence" means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom, including the Isle of Manned the Channel Islands. "Inland Correspondence" is divided into the following classes: (n) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) Postcards (e) Parcels (c) Halfpenny paclets Not only letters, .but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size. LETTER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows : Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... ld. For every additionai 2 0z5.... id. A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufficiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one foot in depth._

POST-CARDS. The prepaid rate of postage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on • every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable oa delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 51 inches in length by 3i inches in width, or be less than 31 inches in length by 2± inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and finch wide) bearing the address may now be affixed to the address side of card. If any of the rules relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. HALFPENNY PACKET POST. The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 2oz. in weight. Printed matter, Sze., not in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST. .

The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is J. Newspapers not registered at the General Post Office are treated in the pcst like any other printed matter. • .

PARCEL POST. The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: Fora parcel ... ... not excieeding 1 lb. 0 3 lf exceeding 1 lb. ?I 2 lb. 0 4 71 „ 2 lb. ff * 3 lb. 0 5 ?i. ft 3 lb. VP 5 lb. 0 6 ft ft 5 lb. ty 7 lb. 0 7 ft t, 7 lb. fIP 8 lb. 0 8 f f ff 8 lb. ? f 9 lb. 0 9 If If 9 lb. ff 10 lb. 010 10 lb. 11 lb. 0 11 No parcel may exceed 111 b. in weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel in:— Greatest length... ... ... ... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring 3ft. Oin. in its longest dimensions as much as 2ft. 6in. round its thickest part; a short parcel may be thicker; thus, if it measure no more than 3ft, in length, it may measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid. Mark each parcel" Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving.

PARCELS -(FOREIGN AND COLONIAL). Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid. AIONN ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which thoy are made payable, unless they are cros<ed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a id. can be contnined in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 2d. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 4d.; £2O, 6d.; £3O, Bd. ; £4O, 10d. By paying an additional 2d. and cost of the telegram of advice, a money order can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order may be issued for places abroad varies in each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. ; £2, Gd. ; and above that, 3d. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A fee of 6d. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS.

These are obtainable at any money order office in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money otdors: that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can ho paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by Gd., from Gd. to £1 is. The poundage fees are up to 2s. 6d.; Id. up to 155.; lid. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General will entertain no further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to make up odd sums. TELEGRAMS (INLAND). The Patmaster-General possesses,al most entirely, the privilege of transmitting,collecting and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. The charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of 6d. wlPich covers 12 words, and an additional 16. for each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a•sub-ollice then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made from the office door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, but sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be 'prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or banded to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost 9f registering an abbrevi- ated addrev, which must consist of two words, is £1 is. TELEGRAMS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL) Must be written on special forms and are accepted in the language of any of the principal European 'countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANK. These are established at all money order offices. In ordinary cases the annual deposit must not exceed £5O, and the total amount £2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children under seven years of age cannot be withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps affixed to a special form. Each depositor receives a bank book in whibh the amount of each deposit should be entered at the thus of paying in. If the deposit is £1 or over, a special acknowledgment is sent from the head London office. Interest is allowed at the rate of .2)t per cent, Any amount up to and including LI may be withdra-an without previous notice, on presenting a bank book. The bank book should be sent each year, postage free, on the anniversary of the date when the account was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Bank Department for purposes of checking and adding interest. The withdrawal of money can bee xpedited by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost of telegrams. P.O. TELEPHONE AND TRUNK LINE CHARGES.

MOTOR, CAR AND CYCLE REGULATIONS.

_ Each one must be registered with the council of a county or county borough, the fee being .£1 bred motor car, and ss. for a motor cycle. A register a number is assigned to each, which it is an offence not to keep affixed when driving. Each driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from the county authorities, which may be endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other nffences. The highest speed at which a car my be driven on the public highways is 20 miles an- hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions. It is an offence under the Motor Car Act (1903), to drive a car in such a way on a public highway as to be dargerot-3 to the public, hiving regard to all the circumstances of the case. In the event of an accident to any person due to bo presence of the motor or on the road, the river must supply his name and address, and the twner and number of the car, under heavy penal- odie The-driver must produceh is licence at the sequest of a. police constable.

Warrington Easily Beat. Leigh.

The Christmai Day fare attracted a crowd of 5,000 to Wilderspcol, and the locals put up a fine display against their visitors from Leigh. Beautiful weather prevailed, and with the ground in good order, there was some very fast play on both sides. Warrington were quite at full strength, while the visitors had also a fair side, though such men as T. Johnson and Winstanley were notable absentees. Four men who own Warrington as their native town, appeared in the Leigh ranks, viz., W. Ashton, Leah, Attewell and Gallop. Both sides set to work with a will at the commence- moat of hostilities, and Johnson was early dangerous on the Leigh right. Warrington responded in fine style, and Clarkson cleared brilliantly. The Leigh men were penalised in their own quarter and Fish opened the score with a neat goal. Warrington still continued to claim the bulk of the play and another brilliant effort on the right by Trehearne, who passed inside to Brooks, led up to Brooks scoring the first try, Fish adding the extra points. Warrington continued to play a strong game and, singular to add, the second try was almost an emulation of its predecessor, but this time Taylor put on the finishing touches to Trehearne's fine play, and Fish added his third goal. For a few minutes the excitement toned down somewhat, and though Leigh made spasmodic bursts there was no sting in the attack. Not so with Warrington, for clever work by the backs gave P. Boardman a chance to shine, and he crossed the rut, but this time the goal kick was a failure. One of the most exciting incidents during this half was an effort near half-time, for Brooks, kicking from some loose play, indulged in a rare race for the leather with Gallop, and the honours went with the international half-back, Thomas bringing Warrington's record up to twenty-points to nil when the interval was sounded Leigh nil.

Tinning round Warrington early on improved their advantage, for brilliant passing led up to Dickenson scoring, and Thomas added a goal. The homesters now lessened their efforts. Leigh improved spler.didly and they had their just deserts when Johnson put on a try, in the manipulation of which the passing reached a high standard Johnson goaled. Smith next scored after clevet work, but this time there was no goal. A really clover goal from Taylor's foot was followed by Leah scoring a try, and Woodward adding a goal the end of a really fine game was as follows : Warrington, six goals five tries (27 points), Leigh, two goals three tries (13 points.)

Warrington Wirat Widnes.

The Warrington men went to Widnes on Saturday with the avowed intention of avenging the defeat sustained earlier in the season. A full team was avail- able, and the Warrington men were strongly supported by followers from Warrington. There was a gate of 4,000 at Widnes, and the match was played in ideal weather. The teams were as follows:

Widnes—Davies, back ; Ince Lambert, Taylor, and Moran, three-quarter backs ; Lay and Ditchfield, half backs ; G. Aspey, S. Aspey, W. Rigby, F. Rigby, Carr, and M'Carthy, forwards. Warringto.u—Tilley, back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Trehearne, three quarter backs; and Jenkins, half backs • Thomas, P. Boardman, A. Boardman, Neill, Dowel, and Che,sters, forwards. Referee, Mr. Mainwaring (Salford). Touch judges, Messrs. T. Foulkes (St. Helens) and Bennett (Liver- pool). G. Aspey set the ball in motion for the home side, and Dickenson was collared in possession. Widnes pressed but Ike Taylor took a penalty kick with judg- ment and relieved. Lambert snapped ups pass which Dickenson intended for Taylor. He got away nicely, but a speedy Warringtonian overhauled him. Widnes were having rather the better of the game, but Tilley was extremely sound in defence. Fish raised a siege of some minutes' duration with a pretty run. Lally came round the pack too soon and was penalized, with the result that Fish landed a goal. Widnes were playing dashing football now, and Dickenson and Trehearne . were rolled Over in rapid succes- sion. A bad slip by Davies gave the visitors a chance which was spoiled by over eagernes. Sam Aspey headed a capital drnble. He took the ball forty yards and beat Tilley, but Taylor gave chase and managed to send to touch. Judicious work by Tilley, aided by the alert Fish, took the game back to the Widnes "25." Warrington indulged in some grand passing and Trehearne looked dangerous when held. 'filen Taylor dodged several opponents and ran well. Fortunately for the" Chemicals" his pass to Fish was a trifle wide and the wing man failed to take it. Fierce scrimmaging followed, Jenkins eventually hurling him- self over the line. Fish landed a good goal. Widnes 1, had a good share of the game after this, but Tilley was , a stumbling block to the home team. Half-time.— Wsrrington two goals one try (seven points); Widnes nil. ' On resumption Brooks ran weil and Fish supplemented 1 by travelling a long way before the whistle went for a preNious knock on. Fish was not to be held in check, and, a moment later, -he raced away in the old style, j dodging man after .man. When hemmed in he ttansferred to Neill and the Welshman dished over. Thomas j mad a good but unsuccessful bid at goal from a difficult position. Widnes were next to ' shine and carried play to the other end with a desperate burst. Tilley acknowledged the effort by conceding a touch down. Widnea again pressed, and, after some exciting play, the Warrington Lack was held over his own lime. The ball was got away safely, Dickenson's defence being sound. After a dull period Lambert got going again but the impregnable Tilley stopped him. Thomas and another Warrington forward dribbled away past Ditehfield and seemed certain to score, when Thomas knocked-on in trying to pick up; Harry Taylor punted loftily over the" Wire pullers" lineand Tilley Jet the ball go dead. Widnes were getting possession well from the pack and had a spell of attacking. Fish was winded in a tackle but speedily recovered. Widnes wee pressing a couple of minutes from the close, when a sensational piece of play occurred. The. ball was sent to Davies who kicked straight to Dickenson. The Warrington centre dashed away at a great rate towards centre. When he was challenged he threw to Fish, and the wing ran over from half way with the try of the match, George Thomas fulled with the goal kick. Result :—Warrington, two goals three tries (13 points); Widnes, nil.

THE ALLEGED WHOLESALE RAILWAY

THEFTS.

Prisoners Again Remanded.

At the Borongh Police Court, on Tuesday morning, before 11. Roberts and other justices, John Morton, wiredrawer, 13, Monks-street ; and Thomas Walton, engine-driver, 13. Monks-street, were charged on remand with stealing and receiving from a van in transit on the London and North-Western Railway on Sunday, December 20th, a bale of leather, the property of the railway company; also"with stealing and receiv- ing from a van on December 19th, one cheese and 32 packets of jelly, valued at £1 10s. Gd. ; also with steal- ing and receiving from a van on December 2ad, 154 pads of silk gauze, valued at £2. Walton and Edwin Hamer, 15, Gaskell-street, were charged with stealing and reciving from a van in transit on October 16th, a quantity of men's and boy's clothing, the property of the company. The Chief Constable stated that unfortunately the solicitor for the prosecution had been "snowed up," and had been unable to get there. Mr. Steel, who was defending Walton, also wished the Bench to he informed that he was "snowed up." The prosecution wished that application should be made for a further adjourn- ment for seven days. The railway police, had been unable to clear the matter up, and they desired a further remand. There was a considerable quantity of property stolen, and having regard to the fact that the past week had been one of holidays, the railway com- pany had bad great difficulty in tracing ownership. A remand for seven days was granted.

LIFE-BOAT SERVICES IN 1908.

Although the storms and gale; of the year 1903 pro- vided plenty of work for the life-boats of the Royal National Life-Boat Institution and their gallant crews the year, as a whole, was not a remarkably boisterous one. The heaviest gales of the year occqrred on the 6th-Bth March and the 31st August,-ist September. On the first occasion 62 lives were saved and 5 vessels, and 13 other persons were landed, 19 life-boats being launched; whilst during the other gale mentionerl, 28 life-boats were launched, resulting in the saving of 7 lives and 1 vessel, 59 persons in imminent danger being also landed. Between the Ist January and the 31st December, inclusive, the Institution's life-boats were launched on service 407 times in addition to which crews were assembled in readiness for service on 55 occa- sions, wheir their help was not ultimately required. The total number of Ryes saved in 1908 by the life-boats was 393, and by the shore-boats and other means, for which the Institution granted rewards, 152, making a grand total for the year of 515 lives saved. As many as 39 vessels and boats were also saved from total or partial loss. The number of lives for which the Life- boat Institution has granted rewards since it was established on the 4th March, 1821-84 years ago—has cow reached 47,890. The cost of maintaining the institution's large fleet of 280 life-boats in thorough efficiency is very great, and increases ; but the financial support received from the public in annual subscriptions and donations is quite inadequate 'for, the purpose. wurther help is therefore much needed. Annual sub- scriptions and donations will be thankfully received and acknowledged by the Secretary, Charles Dibdin, Esq., 22 Charing Cross-road, London, W.C., by any of the branch honorary secretaries, and by all the bankers in the United Kingdom.

QUESTIONS FOR TARIFF REFORMERS.

Xl.-11 Britain, an old-established Industrial Nation, needs Tariff Reform, how can she adopt it without providing India with a Tariff against British manufac- tures? •

Every year India imports large quantities of manufactures, and the greater part of them is imported from the United Kingdom. The facts are:

_ IMPORTS INTO INDIA (YEAR ENDED maßem 315 t, 19a7.) In Million £ From 'United From Foreign Kingdom. Countries. Food, &c. 2.1 ... 5.7 Raw Materials ... 1-0 ... 2.6 Manufactures ... 50.6 ... 10.2 53.7 18 5

If Protection is true, it is clear that we are doing a deadly wrong to India. Tf we adopt Protection, and do not give India Protection, we shall be cheating India from a Protectionist standpoint. If we adopt Protection, and give India protection, we shall be hurting ourselves in one of our best markets. Which course do the Tariff Reformers suggest that vie should take? L. C. C. M.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

The Infirmary. Sir,—Would the "Examiner" afford a free advertise- ment on behalf of the Warrington Infirmary by the insertion of this letter? Rule No. 9 has recently been altered to the effect that "annual subscribers of 10s. Gd, will be entitled to two recommendation forms "—annually. This alteration especially commends itself to the people residing in the country parishes adjacent to the borougb, rrany of whom will be glad to subscribe 10s. 6d., but who have thought 21s. too much for them, and my object in asking you to publish the fact is that it may become known to the agricultural community generally who I know, so far as their means will allow, are amongst the most generous in such philanthropic and, often to them, useful objects. I have only to add that subscriptions are due on New Year's Day each year.

Yours truly, WM. HOUGHTON. The Grange, Paddington, Jan. Ist, 1909.
