Tariff Reformers and Trade Returns

Sir,—Will you allow me a snail space to reply to my good friend Mr. England on the above subject? lam afraid that I don't quite understand Lis letter. Does he wish to abandon the position hitherto so stoutly defended by Free Traders that all imports most be paid for by exports of goods, or does he concede that they may be paid for by exports of British securities 0.% ned by British subjects ? If Mr. England contends that the excess of imports over exports (as shown by his figures) may be taken to be the measure of our prosperity, so long as our own earning power through invisible exports are not decreased thereby, my reply is as fellows: If the excess of imports over exports is the best test of national prosperity, the years in which the ratio of the trade balance is lowest should be (accordinF, to Mr. England) the least prosperous and the years in which it is highest, the most prosperous. But the trade balance was lowest in 1872 when it was 16 per cent, of the exports, and 1873 when it was 8 per cent. These years by every test were the most prosperous in the annals of British trade. The balance was highest in 1877, when it was 71 per cent, of the exports (and 1877 was a most disastrous year for British trade) and 1898 when it was 75 per cent. of the exports (and 1898 again was a year of singularly bad trade). In IS9O on the other hand, a year of good trade, the balance was low, being only 35 per cent. of the exports. Th6refore Mr. England's assertion that British prosperity is to be ascertained by the excess of imports thus finds no support whatever in facts. So much for my friend's figures. lam afraid, how- ever, that my friend is on the wrong path, if he wishes to prove to the people of Warrington that Free Trade is good. What he wants to do is to account to them for the unemployment. It is no benefit to a man out of work to be shown Board of Trade figures. What he wants to know is how to get work. We Tariff Reformers say that" The fundamental argu- ment for Protection is its benefits to labour." The chief reason that I personally am a Tariff Reformer is that I believe not only in work for all, but a living wage for all. I do not consider 17s. 10d. a living wage, but under the conditions of Free Trade it is impossible for the employers to pay more. In conclusion, I can assure my friend that the time, in my opinion, is not far distant when Tariff Reform will be adopted in England for her benefit.—Yours truly, G. H. WARBURTON STEEL. 31st December, 1908.

The Rurl-Decanal Conference.

Rev. John Yonge's Vigorous Protest. Sir,—Your readers ;would peruse your report of the recent Ruri-Decanal Conference with a good deal of surprise, and perhaps also some little regret that good and worthy ministers of the Christian religion could display such narrowness of feeling and perversity of judgment. I am not disposed, however, to say much about their curious opinions as to the Education question, or their demands about Secondary and Elementary schools in this County Borough. The proper authorities will know how to deal with their projected policy in these matters. Happily we have had, so far, no "religious difficulty "in Warrington, and it is to be hoped that clerical indiscretions, such as have recently been dis- played, will no.t seriously disturb the tranquility which hitherto has prevailed. If, however, any such disturb- ance does arise, everyone will know who its creators have been. Usually, the " dissenters " have been the alleged disturbers of the peace. In this case, they, at least, are not to blame. My special object in these lines is to enter an earnest protest against the extraordinary speech of the Rev. J. Carson. One wonders very much where Mr. Carson gets his ideas of Nonconformists from, and especially where he finds any foundation whatever for his opinions about Nonconformist ministers ! He is reported to have said in his speech that "so far as he could make out from the sermons of Nonconformist ministers, they kad •no Christianity at all And then he went on to support that astounding assertion by a reference to "a particular instance which had come under his own personal observation in which the Saviour was left out, and His atoning sacrifice, and the great work He came on earth to do, and Ho wais looked upon merfaly as being a good religious Teacher." Is Mr. Carson so devoid of commonsense and common fairness as to infer that because one solitary sermon which has "come under his own personal observation "may warrant the description he gives of it, all the sermons of all Non- conformist ministers are. of the same character ? Surely Mr. Carson's acquaintance with sermons other than his own and those of his fellow clergy, must be almost "nil." He might do worse, in the matter of current sermon literature, than take for a few weeks the "Christian World Pulpit" (price one penny), in which he would find every week verbatim reports of sermons pinched both by eminent Anglicans and by representative and " orthodox " Nonconformists. From the perusal of these it is certain he would speedily learn the untruth and unwisdom of his wholesale denunciation of Nonconformist preachers as having "no Christianity at all !." Does Mr. Carson seriously mean to say that he actually believes that Methodist, Baptist, Presbyterian, to say nothing of Congregational ministers have "no Gospel ?" if he does mean that, is it not time that, in justice to his own character as a Christian minister believing in truth, be should take pains to inform himself of the actual facts of the matter? Any Nonconformist minister who preaches as Mr. Carson describes,has but a minority along with him, either of preachers or of people. lam confident that the Evangelical Nonconformist ministers of this district would repudiate the specimen of Non- conformist preaching as accurate in their own cases, and that they feel no little indignation that an Angl:can clergyman can so far forget his own dignity and so latterly misrepresent the well known facts of the day as to declare that Nonconformist ministers "have no Christianity at all " ! I confess that it is also an addi- tional surprise to me that members of that Ruri-decanal Conference with whom I have the frequent pleasure of co-operating in various ways, could suffer Mr. Carson's absolute misrepresentation to pass unchallenged. Why, Sir, the assertion is one of astounding untruth! Has Mr. Carson never heard of eminent Nonconformist theologians, such as the late Dr. Dale, of Birmingham, whose work upon "The Atonement" has long been a text book in orthodox schools of Theology, Anglican, and Nonconformist alike : or my own college tutor, the late Dr. Henry Robert Reynolds, of Cheshunt, or Dr. Fairbairn, still the Principal of Mansfield College, Oxford ? has Mr. Carson in his quiet retreat at War- grave, never heard of Charles Haddon Spurgeon, whose " Gospel" no sane man has ever questioned, and whose " message " to saint and sinner gained for him a supreme name among Nonconformist preachers? I suppose be may have heard of one John Clifford, D.D., who, whatever his extreme (?) opinions in matters of educational freedom may be, has an unquestioned and unrivalled rank among Nonconformist preachers of "Jesus Christ and Him Crucified." Perhaps he has never beard of J. H. Jowett, Dr. Dale's successor at , Carr's Lane, Birmingham,or of the late Charles Garmtt, of Liverpool, or of Scott Lidgett, President of the ' Wesleyan Conference, or of John Watson, D.D., of

===== 0002601_19090102_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
Felton Park, the personal friend of the Bishop of Liver-I pool. But he has come to personal knowledge of one nameless Nonconformist "who has no Christianity at all." And from such slender, infinitesimal origin he shapes a missile to hurl, with all his oratorical force, against the host of orthodox Nonconformists who out- number all Anglicanism—High, Broad, and evangelical! It seems to me singularly fortunate that the sayings and doings of this marvellous Ruri-decanal Conference have been so ably reported. I note with pleasure that the Rector of Warrington strove to curb the impetuosity of some of his brethren. It is to be hoped that he will succeed in saving them from a withering repulse, if they endeavour to cajole such a body of Managers as control ' the Arpley-street school to give " right of entry "to a "religious teaching" which the " People's College" has never included in its curriculum from the beginning of its career.—Faithfully yours

JOHN YONGE, 26, Ellesmere-road, Stockton Heath, December 29th, 1908.

A Discovery at the Parish Church. 1 Sir,—What an age of discovery is this, to be sure! It is encouraging to know Warrington is not behind in the art of exploration. The December number of the "Warrington Parish Church Magazine" came into my hands yesterday, and within its columns I note the " discovery " to which I refer. The name of the fortunate finder does not appear but, under the heading "The use of the Stole" I read : " For nearly twenty years the altar has been rightly vested according to the season and with what we believe to be the proper colours . For the last ten years or more we have followed suit with stoles and have worn them at all times of our ministration according to the season, but investigation seems to show that we are wrong and we find now that there is no authority for wearing stoles on every occasion, and that they should properly be restricted to Sacramental uses, follow- ing the colour of the season only at the Holy Eucharist. We intend therefore at Advent to dis- continue theuse of the stole at matins and even- song and use it for Sacramental purposes only, viz., for the administration of Holy Baptism and celebration of the Holy Communion, for the solemnisation of Holy Matrimony and in the exercise of penance." This is most interesting, as showing the attitude of mind prevailing at the Parish Church ! Has it taken ten years to discover that the use of the stole in a Protestant Church is a violation of authority? How long has the English Church authorised the "Sacramental use of Penance ? " Article xxv. declares that "Confirmation, Penance, Orders, Matrimony, and Extreme Unction are not to be counted for Sacraments of the Gospel, being such as have grown partly of the corrupt following of the Apostles, partly are states of life allowed in the Scriptures, but yet have not like nature of Sacraments with Baptism and the Lord's Supper, for that they have not any visible sign or ceremony ordained of God." These words are taken as the authorative teaching of the English Church. Why, then, do the local clergy place these pre-Reformation customs and practices on a level with the two authorised Sacraments? Apparently there are still in Warrington, members of the National Church who practice auricular confession and penance—(for they are intimately associated)—and then some one asks "where is the need for Emmanuel Church?" But, Sir, are not these customs dragging our holy religion into contempt? The average Englishman has neither time for nor interest in the hair-splitting of sacerdotal law-makers or law-breakers. He reveres his God and honours the Christ, and looking to the great Church .9f England asks "Who and what is the God you worship? Is the richness of His blessings and Lene.dictions dependent upon, or enhanced by artistic vesting of altars' and the garments of Priests? Is He really influenced by these performances ?" What becomes of the Apostolic declaration that" your bodies are the temples of the Holy Ghost "and that the members of the Church are to he" filled with all the fullness of God "—since Christ " dwells in your hearts by faith"? A great deal might be said as to the Pagan origin and significance of these things (in which I am much inter- ested having been brought up in the extreme wing of the sacerdotal party), but for the present, I express the hope that fresh discoveries will yet be made, and that presently we may all come and join hands in the practical expres4ion of the faith we so often profess in song.

"Thou oh Christ, art all I want ! More than all in Thee I Gad."

Yours &c., FRANK VAUGHAN. Emmanuel Church, December 315t,1008.

YESTERDAY'S FOOTBALL.

Warrington Beat Runcorn at Wilderspool.

A DESPERATE STRUGGLE.

There was a popular fixture at Warrington yesterday when Runcorn paid their annua visit to Wilderspool. Thanks to the rapid thaw, the ground was in fairecondi- tion, though very soft. A large crowd assembled and there must have been quite 6,000 people on the ground. Both sides were representative, though Brooks, the international half-back, stood down from the home side at the last minute, and the vacancy was filled by Lloyd. Runcorn were short of Butterworth and Hughes. The teams lined out as follows :

Warrington.—Tilley, full back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor and Treharne, threequarters ; Lloyd and Jenkins, half backs • Thomas, O'Neill, Dowell, P. Boardman, Belton, aneChester, forwards. Runcorn—Harrison, full back ; Deakin, Unsworth, Moran and Flanagan, threequarters; Jolley and Watton, half backs ; Walker, Richardson, Padbury, Kennedy, McDonald and Dixon, forwards. Warrington started the game through Thomas and the visitors were early on the attack, Tilley's kick being charged down. Flannagan hada clear colirse but Jenkins intervened and a penalty saved the home side. Then Fish got hold from a weak punt by Watton, and put in a beautiful run before passing to Dickenson. The latter kicked and Runcorn at once get the ball down again. Tilley being tackled by Watton. Runcorn had a penalty and Padbury made a shocking attempt at goal. Runcorn tried the kick and rush game to some purpose, 11 but Fish again drove them out. The Linnets were I dangerous in their peculiar style, and after Tilley had been collared again Taylor kicked dead* under the bar. Warrington were not showing their best form, and the visiting forwards followed up with speed, and hot work in the home quarter followed. Tilley was again weak and missed a catch. Walker dashed over for Runcorn but the whistle had gone for a penalty a reconcl before, and Warrington escaped. But it was only temporary. Fine forward play by Runcorn saw the home defence beaten by Padbury and Walker, the former scoring. The goal kick failed. So far Warrington had been second best but Taylor led the way to the Runcorn quarter, *here Fish was overwhelmed near the corner. Fish bad a long range penalty kick and sent the ball under the bar. Again Warrington got away on the left but Harrison made no mistake with Fish. A beautiful passing run by Taylor and Treharne looked certain to materialise, but Taylor missed the final transfer near the line* Warrington were now playing finely and Thomas nearly rushed the ball over. Treharne was next sent in touch at the corner after a fine run by O'Neill and Jenkins. Then Taylor cut in and when tackled passed to Fish who rushed eve-, and gave his side the lead with a nice goal and deafening cheers. Another thrill was pro- vided by Jenkins who kicked over the Runcorn line and the Linnets only just touched down in time. Jolley next tripped Taylor and was cautioned by the referee. Runcorn attacked again but Jolley was penalised close in and Taylor cleared. Then Walker came ita for a caution for roughness. Half-time: Warrington, 5 points; Runcorn, 3 points. Richardson restarted the second half of a desperate struggle. The first incident was a fine forward dribble by Warrington, which took play to the line, Richardson saving. Runcorn were penalised in front of the goal and Fish again sent the ball over the bar. Harrison was carried of! injured, Jolley taking his place. Fish shone in some exchanges, but Runcorn were aggressive, Watton being held by Jenkins. Harrison now resumed and the Linnets attacked strongly, Padbury missing a penalty kick in front of goal. After even play Flannagan made a long run which was checked by the ball hitting the referee. Runcorn attacked hotly and liusworth was nearly in. Lloyd relieved and Treharne ran well into the Runcorn half. The Linnets were up again in a moment and from a short penalty kick ‘Vatton scored. Padbury made a poor attempt at goal and Warrington were still a point ahead. It was now Warrington s turn and Fish was held at the corner from Lloyd's pass. Then Jenkins threw out and Taylor trick- ing the Runcorn defence scored a beauty under the bar, Fish again goaling. The next moment, Taylor put in a brilliant run and tackled Harrison. It was a ding dong game and there was little between the teams. Walker intercepted a pass and had a clear course, but he was short of speed. Runcorn made the pace hot and Jolley kicked a penalty goal. Warrington at once rushed the hall to the Runcorn end, where Jolley saved luckily. Taylor again ran grandly nearly to the line whe:e fierce play followed. Warrington hold tightly to their advantage and just on time Taylor took a long drop at goal, which just missed. Final : Warrington 3 goals, 2 tries, 12 points; Runcorn 1 goal, 2 tries, 8 points. RUNCORN "A" v. WAIIRINGT-ON "A."

This mstch shculd have takea place yesterday at Runcorn, but it RAS postponed owing to the ground being unfit.

Honour was done to the memory of an artist who had close associations with Warrington—the late Nlr.George Sheffield—by the holding of an exhibition of his works at the Municipal Art Gallery, in I'day. Over 170 pictures illustrating the artist's many and varied styles of work were got together and in opening the exhibition on May 12th Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P. paid a striking tribute to Sheffield, particularly emphasising the breadth of vision and force of imagination which were two of his greatest qualities.

The Garden Stiburbs scheme, which is fraught with such great possibilities for the well-being of the com- munity, has made an important forward move this year. The company has secured two admirable sites at Great Sankey and Grappenhall and Thursday, October Ist, witnessed the laying of the corner stones of the first four houses to be erected on the Sankey estate. The event was favoured with an ideal diy, there was a largo and representative attendance, and this happy beginning should augur. well for the ultimate sucoess of

the enterprise. The chief ceremonies of the day wore performed by Lady Greenell, as representing the local interest in the movement, and Mr. Ebonzer oward,the Sounder of the. Garden City and the pioneer of the great ideal Whla& is Making such marked progress thoughout the country. The subject of how best to alleviate genuine cases of distress has occupied special attention, and a meeting was held at the Town Hall on October 13th to con- sider the formation of a Civic League of Help—" to awaken the civic and communal conscience with regard to the existence of preventable poverty and misery and to establish a system of communal action for the purpose of mitigating or alleviating the same. After several interesting addresses, a Provisional Com- mittee was appointed to make investigations. After twenty-eight yeaz s' service as a member of the Warrington Police Force Superintendent Monaghan has retired on pension. He has distinguished himself as a particularly smart detective officer, while his geniality has won the goodwill of all. On November sth he was the recipient of an address a gold watch and chain with pendant, a gold ring arid other articles as a testi- monial from the townspeople, while the Borough magis- trates showed their regard and appreciation by presenting him with a smoker's cabinet. Police- Inspector Smith retired on November 20th after 26 years' excellent service in Warrington and Cheshire. In the realm of sport the past year has not witnessed much of exceptional interest. The football season which ended on May 2nd was not one of great achieve- ments for the Warrington Club. Half-a-dozen cups were competed for but not one fell to the prowess of the Wirepullers and they had to surrender the Northern Union Cup gained the previous year. Warrington, how- ever, did fairly well in nearly every competition, winning 23 out of 37 matches. The outstanding event of the present season has been the visit of the Australians (" The Kangaroos ") who were beaten at Wilderspool to the tune of ten points to three, after a hard game. Subsequently the visitors were entertained at the Lion Hotel when the speakers included Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P., vvho spoke highly of the distinctions gained by the Colonials in various branches of sport. The final for the Warring- ton Works Competition, which was decided on May 2nd, resulted in a victory for Rylands' Bros. over Crosfield's by twelve points to three. The Warrington Cricket Club failed to gain League honours and out of 21 matches played they won seven, lost aight, rind six were drawn or abandoned. Although their League achievements wore somewhat mediocre, they proved themselves capable of holding their own against Some of the best teams in the district.. Cresswell, the pro- fessional, carried off the prize offered by the West Lancashire League for the best bowling analysis, for the third year in succession. The Works Cricket Com- petition was won by the Pearson and Knowles team who defeated Walker's Brewery without much difficulty. The ancient game of bowls continues to increase in popularity in Warrington and the town has become quite famous of late for the excellence of its bowling talent. The Bull's Head team had a remarkably success- ful season. In the final of the Lancashire and Cheshire Club championship they were opposed to their old rivals, the Cemetery team, and after an exciting encounter, succeeded in beating them by 21 points. The championship of the Warrington and District League vvas also carried off by Bull's Head who accounted for Rylands' Bros. by 20 points. P. Ellison, a well-known member of the Cemetery team, had the honour of winning the individual merit championship of Lancashire and Cheshire The county prizes were distributed by Mr. A. 11. Crosfield, M.P. (the president) at the annual dinner of the Lancashire and Cheshire Association held in Manchester on October 31st,and with Warringtonians figuring so prominently in the prize list it was especially appropriate that they should receive their awards at the hands of the Borough Member. The Warrington Works Bowling Association has done splendid work in encouraging the game amongst the employees of the local works and the Whitecross team is to be congratulated on winning the championship for the second year in succession. The final with L. and N.-W. was one of the keenest ever witnessed on a Warrington green, the wire men proving the victors by the narrow margin of 7 points. There have been numerous events of interest in the religious world. The pastorate of Golborne-street Baptist Church, rendered vacant by the removal to Liverpool of the Rev. H. Dunnico, was filled in Febru- ary by the armointment of the Rev. R. E. Letheren, who came to Warrington with a record of much useful work at IS'ingsthorpe. The recognition meeting was held on March 20th and was an encouraging inaugura- tion of what is .likely to prove a most successful ministry. The Primitive Methodists of the Liverpool District held their Synod at Warrington in May and the representatives, who came from circuits covering a very large area,were received by 'he Mayor (Alderman Smothnrst). The Rev. G. W. Dickinson was instituted and inducted to the living of Christ Church, Padget°, on May 12th. A particularly notable event was the pre- sentation of a portrait and illuminated address to the Rey. Canon Penrhyn, Rector of Winwicic, on May 22nd, in celebration of his 80th birthday and 50 years in holy orders. A Warrington branch of the Young Women's Christian Association was formed on May 25th. A handsome new organ was opened at Penketh Wesleyan Church on September 10th. The newly-appointed ministers of the Warrington Wesleyan Circuit—the ROTS. E. Blanshard Keeling, Thomas Gladding and F. Welwyn Kernick—received a hearty greeting at the circuit recognition meeting on Septem- ber 17th. Dr. Campbell Morgan, the eminent preacher, visited Wycliffe Congregational Church on October 16th, and preached two powerful sermons. The Rev. John Yonge, the respected pastor of Wycliffe Church, celebrated his seventieth birthday on October 23rd, and tbe occasion was fittingly recognized at Wycliffe by the holding of an" At Home" and congratulatory meeting. The new Church of St. Marsaret and All Hallows' erected at Orford at a cost of £l,OOO, was consecrated by the Bishop of Liverpool on October 31st, and the Rev. G. Vale Owen was instituted to the benefice on December 14th. The Rev. J. F. Matthews, the energetic pastor of Latchford Baptist Church, was on November 30th unanimously chosen for the pastorate of Glossop-road Baptist Church, Sheffield, and accepted the invitation. He has been a force in the Free Church life of Warrington, and be will leave the town with the sincere good wishes of friends of all denominations. The Very Rev. J. P: Whittle, 0.5.8., Rector of St. Alban's, Warrington, celebrated the jubilee of his monastic profession on December 15th, and was pre- sented with an illuminated address and a purse contain- ing a cheque for £l4O. THE INFIRMARY BALL.

Successful Function at the Parr Hall.

The Parr Hall was on Tuesday evening the scene of a highly successful function—the annual ball in aid of the funds of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary. Notwithstanding the wintry weather and the heavy state of the roads, which made it impossible for a number of ladies and gentlemen living outside the town to be present, the attendance was well up to the average' and it is hoped that the receipts, includ- ing special donations, will establish a record. The need for increased funds was never more urgent, for the enlargement of the institution will, it is estimated, entail an annual expenditure of -1,500; and the assured income is only ;£B5O. The annual ball is one of the means by which this heavy liability 1$ met,. and a special appeal on behalf of this deserving edort has met with an _encouraging response. Admirable arrangements for the ball had been made by a ladies' committee consisting of the following: —Mrs. Pickrnere (president), the Mayoress (Mrs. Forshaw), Lady Greenai, Mrs. Airey, Mrs. Armitage, Mrs A. S. Bleckly, Mrs. Broadbent, Mrs. J. D. Buckton, Mrs. Coxon, Mrs. George Dakin, Mrs. Edelsten, Mrs. Edridg,e, Mrs. aohnFairclough, Mrs. Henry Gmeenall, Mrs. Hutton, Mrs. Long, Miss Massy, Mrs. Monks, Mrs J. Charlton Parr, Mrs. H. V. Pigot, Mrs. W. P. Rylancls, Mrs John White, and Mrs. Willis. The stewards were :—Captain Appleton Dr. Bowden, Major Bundock, Colonel Edelsten, Appleton, Edridge, Lieutenant-Colonel Fairclough, Dr. Edward Fox, Mr. H. Greenall, Mr. Roger C. Parr, and Mr. W. Peter Rylands.—A special word of praise is due to Captain G. R. Crosfield, the hon. secretary of the Ball Committee, for his indefatigable labours to promote the success of the function.

Mr. Ralph Johnson provided a special dancing floor, which added much to the comfort and enjoy- ment of the dancers, and he was responsible for the artistic decorations, the hall presenting s beautiful picture. An admirable programme of dance music was submitted by Mr. W. P. Bayley's band, of Liverpool, which had been augmented for the occasion. The Winmarleigh Cafe Company had charge of the catering which gave every satisfaction. The heating of the hall, thanks to Mr. Powis, the hall keeper, was perfect. It was a matter for regret that owing to indisposi- tion Mrs. Pickmere, the esteemed president of the Ladies' Committee, was unable to attend. THE CHARGE AGAINST MR. LOUIS

HENSHALL.

Another Remand.

The Warrington Police Court was crowded on Monday when Mr. Louis Henshall, Runnymede, Stockton Heath, director and manager of Messrs. R. A. Naylor, Ltd., timber merchau ts, Howley Mills, surrendered to his bail on the charge of stealing £79 Os., the money of the firm. The case has aroused considerable interest in the town and many people were unable to gain admittance to the Court. The Mayor (Alderman Forsbaw) presided, and the other magistrates on the bench were Alderman Tinnion, CounciliGrs Wilkinson and Evans Messrs. S. Wright, H. Thornton, W. Roberts, G. Pilling, A. J. Jolley, T. Locker, and W. Furness.

Mr. P. A. Padtnore, of Messrs. Tatham, Worthington and Co., Manchester, appeared for the prosecution, and said it was not the intention to offer any evidence that day He, therefore, asked for a further remand. Mr. Steel, for the defence, suggested that the case should be adjourned till Friday next, January Bth, and this was agreed to. Mr. Elenshall was then remanded, and _bail was allowed as before, himself in .F.,1,000 and two sureties of £OO.

Street Accident In Sankey-street. On Sunday afternoon, Ann Sankey, a married woman, aged 58 years, residing at 63, Garibaldi- street, was walking alone Sankey-street when she slipped, and severely injured her right leg. P.C. Dennett rendered first aid, and afterwards conveyed her to the Infirmary.

PENSIONS DAY.

FIRST PAYMENTS AT WARRINGTON.

New Year's Day of 1909 *ill have a special claim to remembrance. It brought gladness to the hearts of many thousands of the aged poor' for the long- awaited Pensions Day had arived at last, and those people of seventy and upwards who had been able to satisfy the Pensi ons Committees of the genuineness of their claims had only to present their orders at any Post:Office to receive their first allowance under the beneficent Act of the present Liberal Government. Eight o'clock yesterday morning found the officials at the Warrington Head Post Office, with an adequate supply of silver, ready to attend to all pensioners who chose to claim what is now, happily, their right. It was also the day for the payment of army pensions so that a busy time was in prospect. There was no rush of applicants such as many offices in larger centres experienced,and when an" Examiner" representative arrived on the scene a few minutes before' eight o'clock the office had a deserted appearance, as far as the attendance of the public was concerned. People bent on various business errands soon began to pass to and fro, but no pensioners. At about 8.15, however, a woman of the working-class was seen approaching the building with an air of inquiry and timidity which suggested that the object of her visit was something out of the ordinary. It transpired that she herself was not an applicant but had come to draw a pension for her bed-ridden mother who is eighty years of age. On leaving the office she waxed enthusiastic about the kind and obliging way in which she had been treated by the officials, but was a little crest-fallen on finding that she had omitted one of the necessary formalities and would have to make a journey home to obtain the signature of her mother before the pension could be paid. However, the occasion was worthy of a special effort, for, as she remarked, the ss. would come in very useful to provide" a little nourishment "for the mother, and she cheerfully retraced her steps. Shortly before nine o'clock an old lady, who walked very briskly considering her weight of years, pre- sented an order. To her was to belong the distinctioa of being the first pensioner to be paid at the Warring-- ton office. Her name is Mrs. Eliza Kelly, and she is- eighty-three years of age and resides with her son,. Mr. John Kelly, the caretaker of the District Bank, Sankey-street. She, too, had to make a doable journey, for as she was unable to sign the receipt— there were not many scholars when I was a girl" she told our representative—she had to bring her son as a witness. However, the rules were duly com- plied with and at a quarter-past nine Mrs. Kelly left the office with her ss. pension, proud of the fact that she was "the first on the list" for Warrington. She is remarkably active and well preserved for her - advanced age and few better instances could be afforded of the deserving cases which the Act is intended to benefit. Mrs. Kelly, who is a native of Darleston, Staffordshire, lost her husband many years aqo but she has faced life's difficulties bravely and is now the honoured head of four generations. She brought Ul3 a family of ten but unfortunately only four of her children now survive. Mrs. Kelly can, of course, narrate interesting- recollections of the old days, and altogether she is a thoroughly worthy recipient of Warrington's first pension. The ladies quite on-did the sterner sex in the punctuality of their applications. The second pensioner to receive her ss. was Mrs. Anderson, a widow, 69,. Padgate-lane, who was accompanied by her daughter. She will be 82 years of age on the 14th of this month. Mrs. Sarah Worrall, widow, 26, Howard-street, was the third recipient, her pension also being ss. She was too feeble to attend personally—she has not been "up town"for five years—and the pension was paid to her married daughter on her behalf.

The first male—applicant wa;Yr-.-.William Garnett, 10, Porter-street, Howley, who is 73 years of age. There was some little informality about his order but. when this had been rectified at the Inland Revenue. office he received his ss. and went on his way rejoicing. Mr. Garnett is a most interesting personality. He appears to be hale and hearty, carries himself well, and willingly conversed about his erperiences. He is a widower and lives with a married daughter. Mr. Garnett is a native of" the good old town" and can recall the time when one policeman sufficad to preserve law and order here. He also speaks longingly of those far-off days when the Mersey was a clear stream abounding in fish and he just remembers the- building of the present Warrington Bridge. As a boy he witnessed the launching of a coasting vessel at Howley and later he was present when the ship " Tayleur " started its ill-fated career. Mr. Garnett begin 'work at Messrs. Hadfield's cotton factory at Latchford at an early age.At that time there were nine cotton mills in Warrington but the cotton famine of 1860 was disastrous occurrence for them and only the Cockhedge factory now-survives. In later life Mr. Garnett was greengrocer and it is only during the past twelve months or so that he has not followed any occupation.

TS a suggestion that the pension would no doubt prove a boon, Mr. Garnett readly responded " Yes, it will be very welcome." Every consideration has been shown for the convenience of pensioners, and one evidence of this is that pensions are made piyable at any branch offices Which the recipients may select. This, of course, considerably relieved the pressure at the head office. and accounted for the comparatively small number of applications there.

Lip to last evening 61 pensions had been paid at the head office, totalling ,£l5 2s. MEETING OF THE W.M.INGTON COMMITTEE. A meeting of the Warrington Pensions Committee was held at the Town Hall on Wednesday, the Mayor presiding. Thirty-four additional claims were dealt with as follows • —22 pensions of 53. per week were granted ; one of 3s. per week; one of 23. per week .10 cases adjourned. Up to the present time the Committee have dealt with 522 cases, of which about 80 have been disallowed, mostly on the ground of Poor Law Relief having been received. In some few cases applicants were in possession of an income above the statutory amount of 5:31 10s., and in others. the applicants were unable to prove their age to the satisfaction of the Committee. The pensions allowed total about ..-c.:112 per week. It was resolved. —That the best thanks of the com- mittee be given to the kcal supervisor of Inland Revenue for the courtesy they have experienced in all communications with him, and that they also place on record their appreciation of the work done by the- local pensions officers (Messrs. William Price and J. A. Westcott) whose careful and detailed investigations have greatly facilitated the work of the committee. Messrs. Price and Westeott suitably acknowledged the resolution, and expressed their great indebtedness- to the officials of the Town Clerk's department for the assistance they had afforded.

523,488 JOYFUL HEARTS. A GENERAL SURVEY. All pensioners have been supplied with a book of thirteen coupons, which are payable at the rate of one each week, or in batches if presented within three months ef the face date. It is practicable, therefore, for a pensioner to save up the coupons so as to provide a fund with which to meet a payment as high, in the case of a ss. weekly pension, as £3 55., a maximum of thirteen coupons being presentable at once if matured but not out of date.

It is estimated that the total number of old age pensioners for the United Kingdom up-to-date is 523,488. Even allowing for the abnormal death-rate which naturally occurs among people who have reached or passed seventy years, those figures will he augmented and Mr. Asquith's estimate is that fully 600,000 old pensioners will be receiving State payments during 1909. For these, the Chancellor of the Exchequor allows an expenditure of £6,500,000 during the year, or an average of £125,000 a week. According to these figures the average pension will be about 4s. per head. There has been an enormous demand for silver coinage this week by the Post Office, the pensions being paid in multiple of Is. from ss. downwards. It was calculated that by yesterday morning 26,4251 b. of silver coins, presuming it to be new money, would have been despatched from the Mint. If in shillings, the coins placed one above another would make a ptle consider-- ably over two miles high and numbering 2,114,000. Week by week this extraordinary payment in silver coins will increase.
